Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

So the algorithm is:
1. Read input
2. If array is already non-decreasing, output 0
3. Else, compute final values greedily: 
   - final[0] = a[0]
   - for i from 1 to n-1: final[i] = max(a[i], final[i-1])
4. Compute increments = final[i] - a[i] for each i
5. d_max = max(increments)
6. If d_max == 0, output 0
7. Else, output ceil(log2(d_max + 1))

But we need to compute log2 correctly.

In Python, we can use:
- import math
- math.ceil(math.log2(d_max + 1))

But we need to be careful with float precision.

Alternatively, we can compute it without floating point:
- Find the smallest T such that 2^T - 1 >= d_max
- T = 0
- while (2**T - 1) < d_max:
-     T += 1
- return T

But d_max can be up to 10^9, so we need to be careful about performance.

But T is at most about 30 since 2^30 is about 1e9, so it's safe.

Alternatively, we can use bit_length:
- d = d_max
- if d == 0: 0
- else: T = d.bit_length()  # This gives the number of bits, which is floor(log2(d)) + 1
- But we need the smallest T such that 2^T - 1 >= d
- For example, d=1: 2^T - 1 >= 1 → T=1 → 1.bit_length() = 1 ✓
- d=2: 2^T-1 >= 2 → T=2 → 2.bit_length() = 2 ✓
- d=3: 3.bit_length() = 2 → 2^2-1 = 3 >= 3 ✓
- d=4: 4.bit_length() = 3 → 2^3-1=7 >= 4 ✓

So actually, T = d.bit_length() if d > 0.

Because:
- d.bit_length() = floor(log2(d)) + 1
- We need smallest T such that 2^T - 1 >= d
- For d=1: T=1, 1.bit_length()=1
- For d=2: T=2, 2.bit_length()=2
- For d=3: T=2, 3.bit_length()=2
- For d=4: T=3, 4.bit_length()=3

Yes, because 2^(T-1) >= d when T = d.bit_length(), and 2^(T-1) might be less than d sometimes?

For d=4: 2^(3-1)=4, which is >=4 → T=3
For d=5: 2^(T-1) >=5 → 2^2=4 <5, 2^3=8>=5 → T=4
5.bit_length() = 3 → 3 ≠ 4 → contradiction?

5.bit_length() = 3
2^3 - 1 = 7 >=5 → T=3 would satisfy 2^3 - 1 >=5
But we need 2^T - 1 >= d
For T=3: 2^3 - 1 = 7 >=5 → yes
So T=3 is sufficient.

d=5: need T such that 2^T - 1 >=5 → T=3 (7>=5)
5.bit_length() = 3 ✓

d=6: 6.bit_length() = 3, 2^3 - 1 = 7 >=6 ✓
d=7: 7.bit_length() = 3, 7 >=7 ✓
d=8: 8.bit_length() = 4, 2^4 -1 =15 >=8 ✓

So yes, T = d.bit_length() when d > 0

But what about d=0? We handle separately.

So the algorithm:
- For each test case:
  - Read n and array a
  - Check if non-decreasing: if for all i in [0,n-2], a[i] <= a[i+1], then print 0
  - Else:
      - Compute final values: 
          final[0] = a[0]
          for i in range(1, n):
              final[i] = max(a[i], final[i-1])
      - Compute increments: for each i, inc = final[i] - a[i]
      - d_max = max(increments)
      - print(d_max.bit_length()) if d_max > 0 else 0

Wait, if d_max==0, then we already have non-decreasing, so we should have printed 0 in the non-decreasing check.

So we can skip the d_max>0 check.

Final code:

```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        if a[i] > a[i + 1]:
            non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if non_decreasing:
        return 0
    
    # Compute final values to make non-decreasing
    final = [0] * n
    final[0] = a[0]
    for i in range(1, n):
        final[i] = max(a[i], final[i - 1])
    
    # Compute maximum required increment
    d_max = 0
    for i in range(n):
        inc = final[i] - a[i]
        if inc > d_max:
            d_max = inc
    
    return d_max.bit_length()

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: [1,7,6,5]
- Not non-decreasing because 7>6
- final[0]=1
- final[1]=max(7,1)=7
- final[2]=max(6,7)=7
- final[3]=max(5,7)=7
- increments: [0,0,1,2] → d_max=2 → 2.bit_length() = 2 ✓

Example 2: [1,2,3,4,5] → non-decreasing → 0 ✓

Example 3: [0,-4]
- Not non-decreasing (0 > -4)
- final[0]=0
- final[1]=max(-4,0)=0
- increments: [0, 4] → d_max=4 → 4.bit_length() = 3 ✓

Perfect.

Edge case: what if only one element? n=1
- Non-decreasing always → returns 0 ✓

What if [3,1]? 
- Not non-decreasing
- final[0]=3
- final[1]=max(1,3)=3
- increments: [0, 2] → d_max=2 → 2.bit_length()=2

Can we do it in 2 seconds?
- Need to add 2 to position 1
- Add 2 at second 2 → yes, in 2 seconds ✓

Yes.

So the solution is correct.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        if a[i] > a[i + 1]:
            non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if non_decreasing:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Compute final values to make non-decreasing
        final = [0] * n
        final[0] = a[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            final[i] = max(a[i], final[i - 1])
        
        # Compute maximum required increment
        d_max = 0
        for i in range(n):
            inc = final[i] - a[i]
            d_max = max(d_max, inc)
        
        print(d_max.bit_length())
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array `a` of length `n`
2. At second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any subset of indices (including none)
3. We want to make the array non-decreasing as fast as possible
4. We need to find the minimum number of seconds `T`

Key insights:
- At second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any positions we want
- The values we can add are powers of 2: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ...
- Since we can add to any subset, we can think of this as being able to "fix" certain differences between adjacent elements
- The constraint is that we need to make the array non-decreasing: `a[1] ≤ a[2] ≤ ... ≤ a[n]`

## Key Observations

1. If the array is already non-decreasing, answer is 0
2. For any position `i`, we can use powers of 2 to adjust its value
3. The crucial insight is that we can only add positive values, so we can only increase values
4. For any pair of adjacent elements `a[i]` and `a[i+1]`, if `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to increase `a[i+1]` or decrease `a[i]` - but we can only increase, so we must increase `a[i+1]`
5. To increase `a[i+1]` by some amount, we need to use powers of 2 that sum up to that amount
6. The minimum number of seconds needed is determined by the maximum "deficit" we need to fix across the array

- After second 1: a[3]=6+1=7, a[4]=5 → array: [1,7,7,5] — a[3]=7 > a[4]=5, bad
- After second 2: a[4]=5+2=7 → array: [1,7,7,7] — good.

Yes! This works. We added 1 to position 3 at second 1, and 2 to position 4 at second 2.

So total additions: position 3: 1, position 4: 2.

The amount needed for position 4 is 2, which in binary is 10, so requires 2 bits.

Ah! So the amount that needs to be added to position 4 is not just the initial deficit of 1, but may need to be larger to satisfy the intermediate constraints.

In this case, when we add 1 to position 3, it becomes 7, and position 4 is 5, so we need to add at least 2 to position 4 to make it 7.

So the required addition to position 4 is 2, which requires 2 seconds (since 2 = 2^1).

For position 3, we need to add 1, which requires 1 second.

The maximum number of seconds needed is the maximum over positions of the bit length of the amount we need to add to that position.

In this case, for position 4, we need to add 2, which has bit length 2 (since 2 = 10 in binary, highest power is 2^1), so we need T >= 2.

For position 3, we need to add 1, bit length 1.

So maximum is 2.

In example 3: we need to add 4 to position 2. 4 in binary is 100, highest power is 2^2 = 4, so bit length is 3 (since 2^0 to 2^2, need 3 seconds).

So answer is 3.

In example 2: already non-decreasing, answer 0.

So the algorithm is:
1. If array is already non-decreasing, return 0
2. For each adjacent pair i where a[i] > a[i+1], we need to add (a[i] - a[i+1]) to a[i+1]
3. However, when we add to a[i+1], it might affect the pair (i+1, i+2), so we may need to add more than the initial deficit
4. This suggests a dynamic programming or greedy approach

Actually, we may need to simulate the process. But the sum of n over test cases is only 10^5, so we can do something efficient.

But how to compute the required amount to add to each position?

Let me define f(i) = the total amount we need to add to position i.

From the example, for position 4 in example 1, after we add to position 3, the deficit between position 3 and 4 might increase.

Perhaps we can do a greedy simulation from left to right.

Alternative insight: the minimum T is the maximum, over all positions, of the bit length of the total amount that must be added to that position.

And the total amount that must be added to position i is the sum of (a[j] - a[j+1]) for all j such that j+1 = i, but adjusted for the chain.

Actually, in example 1:
- Initially, for position 3: deficit from (2,3): 7-6 = 1, so we need to add at least 1 to position 3
- For position 4: deficit from (3,4): 6-5 = 1, so we need to add at least 1 to position 4
- But when we add to position 3, if it's by an amount that increases position 3 by x, then the deficit between position 3 and 4 becomes (6 + x) - (5 + y) = 1 + x - y, where y is what we add to position 4.
- To keep non-decreasing, we need 1 + x - y >= 0, so x - y >= -1, or y <= x + 1
- But we also need the deficit between position 2 and 3: initially 7-6=1, after adding x to position 3, it becomes (7) - (6+x) = 1 - x, so we need 1 - x >= 0, so x <= 1
- So x <= 1 and y <= x + 1
- We want to minimize the amount added to position 4, which is y.

To satisfy both, we can set x=1, then y <= 2, and we need y >= 1 to fix the (3,4) deficit.

So minimum y=1, then we need to add 1 to position 4.

But in our simulation, when we add 1 to position 3 and 1 to position 4 at second 1, we get position 3:7, position 4:6, which has 7>6, so still bad.

We need to add at least 2 to position 4 to make it 7.

So after adding 1 to position 3, we have position 3=7, position 4=5, so we need 7 - (5+y) >= 0? No, we need a[3] + added[3] <= a[4] + added[4]

After additions, a[3] = 6 + x, a[4] = 5 + y
We need 6 + x <= 5 + y, so x - y <= -1, so y >= x + 1

Also from (2,3): a[2] + added[2] ≤ a[3] + added[3] → 7 <= 6 + x + added[3] → x + added[3] >= 1

We are only adding to position 3 and 4, so added[3] = x, added[4] = y.

From (2,3): 7 <= 6 + x + 0 → x >= 1
From (3,4): 6 + x <= 5 + y → y >= x + 1

So minimum x=1, then y>=2.

So we need to add at least 2 to position 4.

The amount added to position 4 is at least 2.

2 in binary has bit length of 2 (since 2^1 = 2, so seconds 1 and 2, bit length 2).

So T = 2.

For position 3, we need to add at least 1, bit length 1.

So maximum over positions of bit length of added amount is 2.

In example 3: only position 2 needs to add 4, bit length of 4 is 3 (2^2), so T=3.

In example 2: already non-decreasing, T=0.

So the algorithm is:

1. For each adjacent pair i (0 to n-2), if a[i] > a[i+1], then we need to add (a[i] - a[i+1]) to a[i+1]

3. Actually, from the above, the amount we need to add to position i+1 is not just (a[i] - a[i+1]), but might be larger to satisfy the chain

However, in the derivation above, for a chain, the required amount at each position depends on the previous and next.

We may need to iterate to find the exact amounts.

But notice that the required amount to add to position j is at least the sum of the deficits from the left and right, but it's a system.

Alternatively, we can see that in the final non-decreasing array, for each i, a[i] + added[i] must be <= a[i+1] + added[i+1].

With the initial a, we have constraints.

For each i, we have: a[i] + added[i] <= a[i+1] + added[i+1]

So: added[i+1] - added[i] >= a[i+1] - a[i]

Let d_i = a[i+1] - a[i]

Then: added[i+1] >= added[i] + d_i

This is a recurrence on the added values.

We can set added[0] = 0, then added[1] >= 0 + d_0, added[2] >= added[1] + d_1, etc.

But this is only if we don't have constraints from the right side.

Actually, the constraints are: for each i, added[i+1] - added[i] >= d_i

We can set added[0] = 0, then added[1] >= d_0, added[2] >= added[1] + d_1 >= d_0 + d_1, and so on.

For the last element, there is no constraint on added[n-1] beyond what is required.

But also, we might have constraints from the right side, but since for i=n-2, d_{n-2} = a[n-1] - a[n-2], and added[n-1] >= added[n-2] + d_{n-2}, and there's no constraint after that.

In the recurrence, we have added[i+1] >= added[i] + d_i

With added[0] = 0, then added[1] >= d_0, added[2] >= d_0 + d_1, etc.

Actually, after setting the added values as: added[i] = d_0 + d_1 + ... + d_{i-1} for i>=1, with added[0]=0, then we have added[i+1] - added[i] = d_i, which is exactly what we need.

But is this the only solution? No, we can have added[i+1] - added[i] > d_i, which might allow us to reduce the total amount added, but we are only interested in the minimum T such that the values can be represented as sums of distinct powers of 2.

The values added[i] must be non-negative integers, and each added[i] must be representable as a sum of some of {2^0, 2^1, ..., 2^(T-1)}.

The minimum T such that there exist non-negative integers added[i] satisfying the constraints and each added[i] is at most 2^T - 1 (since sum of powers from 0 to T-1 is 2^T - 1).

But we can choose the added values to be as small as possible in terms of the constraints.

The minimal solution is to set added[0] = 0, added[1] = d_0, added[2] = d_0 + d_1, ..., added[i] = sum_{j=0}^{i-1} d_j

But this might not be possible if d_i is negative, because then added[i+1] >= added[i] + d_i, and if d_i < 0, it might decrease.

For example, if d_i < 0, then added[i+1] >= added[i] + d_i < added[i], so it can decrease.

But we also need to ensure that the values are non-negative.

- So added[2] >= added[1] + 3

 for a fixed T, the values we can add to each position are any integer from 0 to 2^T - 1.

 and we have constraints from the non-decreasing condition.

 for each i, a[i] + added[i] <= a[i+1] + added[i+1]

 so for each i, added[i+1] - added[i] >= a[i] - a[i+1]

 we can check if there exists values added[0], added[1], ..., added[n-1] in [0, 2^T - 1] such that for all i, added[i+1] - added[i] >= d_i

 this is a system of linear inequalities.

 we can solve it with dynamic programming or by setting the values in a way that satisfies the constraints.

 specifically, we can use a greedy approach: set added[0] = 0, then for i from 0 to n-2, set added[i+1] = max( added[i] + d_i, 0 ) and then if added[i+1] > 2^T - 1, then T is too small.

 so the derivation is: for each i, constraint is: added[i+1] >= added[i] - (a[i+1] - a[i])

 and we set the minimal possible added[i+1] = max(added[i] - (a[i+1]-a[i]), 0)

 then we simulate and check if all values are within [0, 2^T - 1]

 this matches the examples.

 therefore, the solution is:

 for each test case:
   n = int(input())
   a = list of n integers
   # check if non-decreasing
   is_non_decreasing = True
   for i in range(n-1):
       if a[i] > a[i+1]:
           is_non_decreasing = False
           break
   if is_non_decreasing:
       print(0)
   else:
       found = False
       for T in range(0, 60):  # T from 0 to 59, since 2^60 is huge
           added = [0] * n
           added[0] = 0
           valid = True
           for i in range(n-1):
               d = a[i+1] - a[i]   # difference in the pair (i, i+1)
               added[i+1] = max(added[i] - d, 0)
               if added[i+1] > (1 << T) - 1:
                   valid = False
                   break
           if valid:
               # also check that all values are in [0, (1<<T)-1]
               for i in range(n):
                   if added[i] > (1 << T) - 1:
                       valid = False
                       break
               if valid:
                   print(T)
                   found = True
                   break
       if not found:
           print(60)

 However, note that in the constraint, we have added[i+1] >= added[i] - (a[i+1] - a[i]), and we set to max(added[i] - d, 0) which is the minimum possible value.

 and we only need to ensure that we can achieve the constraints with values in [0, 2^T - 1].

 by setting the minimum values, if they are within bounds, then there exists a solution (by setting the values to at least the required minimum, and we can increase them if needed, but we are only required to use values in the range, and the minimum values are within bounds, so we can use them or increase to a value in the range).

 since the constraints are lower bounds, and we have a feasible assignment (with the minimal values), and if these values are within [0, 2^T - 1], then we can use that assignment.

 therefore, the solution is correct.

 let's run with the examples.

 Example 1: [1,7,6,5]
   is_non_decreasing? 1<=7 yes, 7<=6? no -> not non-decreasing
   T=0: 2^0-1=0
        added[0]=0
        i=0: d=7-1=6, added[1]=max(0-6,0)=0
        i=1: d=6-7=-1, added[2]=max(0-(-1),0)=1
        i=2: d=5-6=-1, added[3]=max(1-(-1),0)=2
        then check: added[3]=2 > 0 -> invalid
   T=1: 2^1-1=1
        added[3]=2>1 -> invalid
   T=2: 2^2-1=3
        added[3]=2<=3 -> valid, and all values:0,0,1,2 <=3 -> valid, so answer=2.

 Example 2: [1,2,3,4,5] -> non-decreasing, answer=0.

 Example 3: [0,-4]
   not non-decreasing? 0>=-4, but 0 > -4, but we need a[0] <= a[1]? 0 <= -4? no -> not non-decreasing
   T=0: d = -4 -0 = -4, added[1]=max(0 - (-4),0)=4 >0 -> invalid
   T=1: 2^1-1=1, 4>1 -> invalid
   T=2: 3, 4>3 -> invalid
   T=3: 7, 4<=7 -> valid, and added[1]=4<=7 -> answer=3.

 It matches.

 Therefore, I will implement this.

 Note: the range of T is from 0 to about 60, and the total sum of n is 10^5, so the total operations is 60 * (sum of n) = 60 * 10^5 = 6e6, which is acceptable.

 Let's code accordingly.


```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if array is already non-decreasing
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        print(0)
    else:
        found = False
        # T from 0 to 60 should be enough
        for T in range(0, 61):
            max_val = (1 << T) - 1  # 2^T - 1
            added = [0] * n
            added[0] = 0
            valid = True
            
            # For each adjacent pair, compute the minimum added[i+1]
            for i in range(n-1):
                d = a[i+1] - a[i]  # difference in the pair (i, i+1)
                added[i+1] = max(added[i] - d, 0)
                if added[i+1] > max_val:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                # Check all added values are within [0, max_val]
                for i in range(n):
                    if added[i] > max_val:
                        valid = False
                        break
                if valid:
                    print(T)
                    found = True
                    break
        # If not found, should not happen, but just in case
        if not found:
            print(60)
```

for _ in range(t):

    a = list(map(int, input().split()))

    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):

            is_non_decreasing = False
            break

        print(0)
    else:

        for T in range(0, 61):

            added = [0] * n
            added[0] = 0
            valid = True
            
            # For each adjacent pair, compute the minimum added[i+1]
            for i in range(n-1):

                added[i+1] = max(added[i] - d, 0)
                if added[i+1] > max_val:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            if valid:
                # Check all added values are within [0, max_val]

                        break

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array `a` of length `n`
2. At each second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any subset of indices (including none)
3. We want to make the array non-decreasing as fast as possible
4. We need to find the minimum `T` such that after at most `T` seconds, the array becomes non-decreasing

Key observations:
- At second `x`, we add `2^(x-1)` which is a power of 2
- Powers of 2 grow exponentially: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ...
- Each value can be incremented by any combination of these powers
- We can add the same power to multiple positions

## Strategy Analysis

Since we can add powers of 2 to any positions, we need to think about when adjustments are needed.

For an array to be non-decreasing, we need `a[i] <= a[i+1]` for all valid `i`.

If we have a violation at position `i` where `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to increase `a[i+1]` or decrease `a[i]`. But we can only add positive values, so we can only increase elements.

Therefore, we can only fix violations by increasing elements that are too small.

The key insight is that each power of 2 can be used strategically. Since powers of 2 are distinct and we can use them on any positions, we need to determine the minimum number of seconds needed to balance the array.

Since we can only add values (never subtract), we need to increase elements that are too small.

Then we have x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i]

This is a recurrence.

We want to find non-negative x[i] that satisfy x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i] for all i.

We can find the minimal such x[i] by solving this recurrence.

We can set x[0] = 0, then x[1] >= d[0], x[2] >= x[1] + d[1] >= d[0] + d[1], and so on.

- But if d[i] < 0, then x[i+1] = x[i] + d[i] might be negative, but x[i+1] >= 0, so we should set x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])

So this doesn't work.

The constraint from index 1 to 2 is x[1] + 7 <= x[2] + 6 → x[2] >= x[1] + 1

I made a sign error.

From a[i] + x[i] <= a[i+1] + x[i+1]
→ a[i] - a[i+1] + x[i] - x[i+1] <= 0
→ x[i+1] >= x[i] + (a[i] - a[i+1])

So d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1] in the constraint x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i]

d[0] = a[0] - a[1] = 1-7 = -6
d[1] = a[1] - a[2] = 7-6 = 1

Then x[3] >= 1 + 1 = 2

So we have: x[0]=0, x[1]=0, x[2]=1, x[3]=2

- x[0]=0 → bit length 0 (or 0)
- x[1]=0 → bit length 0

So maximum bit length is 2.

Answer is 2. Correct.

So the algorithm is:

1. Read the array a
2. If already non-decreasing, then answer is 0
3. Otherwise, compute d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1] for i from 0 to n-2
4. Set x[0] = 0
5. For i from 0 to n-2:
   - x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])   [since x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i], and x[i+1] >= 0]
6. Actually, from the recurrence: x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i], and we want to minimize the final values, so we can set x[i+1] = x[i] + d[i] if that is >=0, otherwise 0? But we need to satisfy the constraint.

Wait, if x[i] + d[i] < 0, then since x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i], and x[i+1] >=0, we can set x[i+1] = 0.

But is that sufficient? Let me test with example 1 at i=0:
x[0]=0, d[0]=-6, x[0]+d[0]=-6 <0, so x[1] = 0
Then i=1: x[1]=0, d[1]=1, x[1]+d[1]=1 >=0, so x[2]=1
Then i=2: x[2]=1, d[2]=1, x[2]+d[2]=2 >=0, so x[3]=2

This matches our earlier solution.

In example 3: i=0, x[0]=0, d[0]=4, x[0]+d[0]=4>=0, so x[1]=4

Perfect.

So the recurrence is:
x[0] = 0
for i in range(n-1):
    x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])   where d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1]

But is this sufficient to satisfy all constraints?

We have x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i] by construction (because if x[i]+d[i] >=0, then x[i+1] = x[i]+d[i], if negative, x[i+1]=0 >= x[i]+d[i] since x[i]+d[i] is negative)

So the recurrence ensures that for each i, the constraint a[i] + x[i] <= a[i+1] + x[i+1] is satisfied.

Also, since we start with x[0]=0, all x[i] are non-negative.

And we have the minimal adjustments? We might be able to reduce some values, but since the constraints are only inequalities, and we're solving a system of inequalities, this greedy choice might give a minimal solution in terms of the values, but we care about the bit length.

Could there be a solution with smaller maximum bit length?

Suppose in example 1, we try to set x[2] < 1 or x[3] < 2. But from the constraint at (1,2): x[2] >= x[1] + 1, and x[1] >= -6, but in our setting x[1]=0, so x[2] >=1. So minimum x[2] is 1.

Similarly, at (2,3): x[3] >= x[2] +1 >=1+1=2. So minimum x[3] is 2.

So our solution is minimal in the sense that the values are minimal.

Therefore, the bit length of each x[i] will be minimized in this solution.

So we can compute:
- For each i from 0 to n-1, compute x[i] using the recurrence
- Then compute the bit length of each x[i]: if x[i] == 0, bit_length is 0 (or we can define as 0), otherwise bit_length is floor(log2(x[i])) + 1
- Then answer is max over i of bit_length of x[i]

But what if x[i] = 0? bit_length should be 0.

In programming, for x=0, we can define bit_length as 0.

For x>0, bit_length = x.bit_length()

Note: in Python, 0.bit_length() returns 0.

So we can just use x.bit_length() for all x.

Therefore, the algorithm for one test case:

1. Read n and array a
2. If n == 1, then already non-decreasing, return 0
3. Compute d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1] for i in range(n-1)
4. Initialize x = [0] * n
5. x[0] = 0
6. For i in range(n-1):
   - x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])
7. Find T = max(x[i].bit_length() for i in range(n))

But wait, in example 1, x = [0,0,1,2]
- x[0].bit_length() = 0
- x[1].bit_length() = 0
- x[2].bit_length() = 1
- x[3].bit_length() = 2
- max = 2 → correct

Example 3: x[0]=0, x[1]=4 → x[1].bit_length() = 3 → correct

Example 2: all x[i]=0 → max bit_length = 0 → correct

But what about the array [1, 3, 2, 4]?

Let's trace:
a = [1,3,2,4]
d[0] = 1-3 = -2
d[1] = 3-2 = 1
d[2] = 2-4 = -2

x[0] = 0
x[1] = max(0, 0 + (-2)) = 0
x[2] = max(0, 0 + 1) = 1
x[3] = max(0, 1 + (-2)) = 0

Now check constraints:
- a[0]+x[0]=1+0=1, a[1]+x[1]=3+0=3 → 1<=3 ✓
- a[1]+x[1]=3, a[2]+x[2]=2+1=3 → 3<=3 ✓
- a[2]+x[2]=3, a[3]+x[3]=4+0=4 → 3<=4 ✓

Good.

Bit lengths: x[0]=0→0, x[1]=0→0, x[2]=1→1, x[3]=0→0 → max=1

Is there a solution with T=1? Yes, because we can add powers of 2 up to 2^0=1.

We need to add 1 to position 2.

At second 1: add 1 to position 2 → becomes [1,3,3,4] → non-decreasing.

Yes, so T=1 is sufficient.

Our algorithm gives T=1, correct.

Therefore, the solution is:

For each test case:
- Read n and array a
- If n == 1: print 0
- Else:
  - Compute d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1] for i in range(n-1)
  - x[0] = 0
  - for i in range(n-1):
      x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])
  - T = max(x[i].bit_length() for i in range(n))
  - print T

Note: when x[i] = 0, 0.bit_length() is 0, which is correct.

But is this always optimal? We are not minimizing the maximum bit length, but we are computing the minimal adjustments, and since the adjustments are minimal, and any valid adjustment must satisfy the constraints, and the bit length of the adjustments is at least the bit length of the minimal solution, and we are using the minimal solution, then it should be optimal.

Could there be a different assignment of x[i] that satisfies the constraints and has smaller maximum bit length?

Suppose we can reduce some x[i] by using a different distribution. But because the constraints are x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i], and we are setting x[i+1] to the minimal possible (max(0, x[i]+d[i])), any other assignment would have x[i+1] >= that value, so it would be at least as large, or possibly smaller only if we reduce x[i], but if we reduce x[i], it might violate earlier constraints.

In fact, the system has a lower bound on each x[i] given by the recurrence, so our assignment is minimal in value.

Therefore, the bit length of each x[i] in our assignment is the minimal possible for that position, so the maximum bit length is also minimal.

Hence, the algorithm is correct.

Let me code accordingly.

Note: the total sum of n over test cases <= 10^5, so O(n) per test case is acceptable.

Edge: when n=1, no d[i], so we can handle it.

Let me write the code.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        continue
        
    # d[i] = a[i] - a[i+1] for i in range(n-1)
    d = []
    for i in range(n-1):
        d.append(a[i] - a[i+1])
    
    # x[i] is the amount added to position i
    x = [0] * n
    x[0] = 0
    for i in range(n-1):
        x[i+1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])
    
    # Find the maximum bit length among all x[i]
    max_bit_length = 0
    for val in x:
        # For 0, bit_length is 0; for positive integers, bit_length is correct
        bit_len = val.bit_length()
        if bit_len > max_bit_length:
            max_bit_length = bit_len
    
    print(max_bit_length)
```

Let me test with the provided examples.

Example 1: [1,7,6,5]
d = [1-7, 7-6, 6-5] = [-6,1,1]
x[0]=0
x[1] = max(0, 0 + (-6)) = 0
x[2] = max(0, 0 + 1) = 1
x[3] = max(0, 1 + 1) = 2
x = [0,0,1,2]
bit lengths: 0,0,1,2 → max=2 → correct

Example 2: [1,2,3,4,5]
d = [1-2,2-3,3-4,4-5] = [-1,-1,-1,-1]
x[0]=0
x[1]=max(0,0-1)=0
x[2]=max(0,0-1)=0
x[3]=0
x[4]=0
all 0 → max bit length=0 → correct

Example 3: [0,-4]
n=2
d = [0 - (-4)] = [4]
x[0]=0
x[1]=max(0,0+4)=4
bit length of 4 is 3 → correct

All correct.

I think this is the solution.

Note: the problem states that the sum of n over test cases <= 10^5, so even with t up to 10^4, the total n is bounded, so it's efficient.

Final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        continue
        
    # Compute differences a[i] - a[i+1] for each adjacent pair
    d = []
    for i in range(n - 1):
        d.append(a[i] - a[i + 1])
    
    # x[i] is the amount we need to add to position i
    x = [0] * n
    x[0] = 0
    for i in range(n - 1):
        x[i + 1] = max(0, x[i] + d[i])
    
    # Find the maximum bit length of any x[i]
    max_seconds = 0
    for amount in x:
        max_seconds = max(max_seconds, amount.bit_length())
    
    print(max_seconds)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array `a` of length `n`
2. At each second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any subset of indices (including none)
3. We want to make the array non-decreasing in as few seconds as possible
4. We need to find the minimum `T` such that after at most `T` seconds, the array becomes non-decreasing

## Key Insights

1. The amounts we can add are powers of 2: `1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ...`
2. Since we can choose any subset of indices at each second, we can strategically add these powers of 2 to fix violations
3. The key observation is that we need to fix all "inversions" where `a[i] > a[i+1]`
4. When we add `2^(x-1)` to some elements, we're essentially adding different contributions to different positions
5. Since we can only add, we need to ensure that positions with smaller values get enough "boosts" to become larger than or equal to adjacent positions

## Approach

Let me think about this differently:

At each second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any subset of indices. This means we can add arbitrary combinations of powers of 2 to each element.

The crucial insight is that we only need to consider the differences between adjacent elements. For each position `i` where `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to make up the difference.

The amount of "correction" needed at position `i` to fix `a[i] > a[i+1]` is `a[i] - a[i+1]` (we need to either increase `a[i+1]` or decrease `a[i]`, but since we can only add, we can only increase elements).

Wait, we can only add, so we can only increase elements. This means:
- If `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to add to `a[i+1]` to make it at least as large as `a[i]`
- But we can also add to `a[i]`, which would make it worse

So actually, if `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to add to `a[i+1]` to make it ≥ `a[i]`.

But we can add at different seconds with different power-of-2 coefficients. The total amount we can add to any position is the sum of `2^(x-1)` for all seconds `x` where we choose that position.

The key insight is that we can represent any non-negative integer as a sum of distinct powers of 2 (binary representation). So for any positive amount we need to add to a position, we can achieve it with a certain number of seconds.

However, we need to fix all adjacent violations simultaneously.

Actually, let me reconsider the constraints. Since we add the same power of 2 to multiple positions at each second, and we want the final array to be non-decreasing, we need to find the minimum `T` such that we can assign binary values (0 or 1) to each position for each second `x` (from 1 to T), such that after all additions, the array is non-decreasing.

For each position, the total added value is the sum over seconds `x` of `2^(x-1)` times whether we chose that position at second `x`.

So the total amount added to position `i` is a sum of distinct powers of 2, which means it can represent any non-negative integer value.

Therefore, for each adjacent pair where `a[i] > a[i+1]`, we need to ensure that the total amount added to position `i+1` is at least `a[i] - a[i+1]`.

The question becomes: what is the minimum `T` such that we can assign additions (powers of 2) to positions to satisfy all these constraints?

But we can share the same seconds across positions, so the constraint is that for each constraint `add_to[i+1] ≥ a[i] - a[i+1]`, we need to be able to achieve this amount with additions that come from seconds 1 to T.

Since any non-negative integer can be represented as a sum of distinct powers of 2, the only constraint is whether we can achieve the required amounts without conflicting with other constraints.

Actually, the minimum T needed is determined by the maximum "gap" we need to fill across any adjacent pair.

Wait, but we can handle multiple gaps simultaneously. The key insight from the examples:

Example 1: `[1, 7, 6, 5]`
- Position 1 to 2: 7 ≥ 1 ✓
- Position 2 to 3: 6 < 7 → need at least 1 more to position 3
- Position 3 to 4: 5 < 6 → need at least 1 more to position 4

So we need to add at least 1 to position 3 and 1 to position 4.

We can do this in 2 seconds: at second 1, add 1 to positions 3,4; at second 2, add 2 to position 4 (but that makes position 4 = 5+1+2=8, position 3=6+1=7, so [1,7,7,8] which is non-decreasing).

We could also add 1 to position 4 at second 1, and 1 to position 3 at second 1, and then no more additions needed. But we can only add powers of 2.

Actually, in second 1 we can add 1 to any subset. So we can add 1 to both positions 3 and 4 at second 1. Then at second 2, we can add 2 to position 4 (or to any position). 

But we don't need to add at second 2 if we can make it work with just second 1. Let's check: after adding 1 to positions 3 and 4:
- a[3] = 6+1 = 7
- a[4] = 5+1 = 6 → now 7 > 6, so not non-decreasing!

So we need more. If we add 1 to both position 3 and 4 at second 1: a = [1,7,7,6] → now 7 > 6 at positions 3,4.
Then at second 2, we add 2 to position 4: a[4] = 6+2 = 8 → a = [1,7,7,8] → now it's non-decreasing.

So T=2.

The necessary amount to add to position i+1 when a[i] > a[i+1] is (a[i] - a[i+1]). We need to add this amount to position i+1.

Since we add powers of 2, we need the binary representation of these values. The maximum power of 2 needed among all the gaps determines T.

In this example, we need to add 1 to position 3 (from 6 to 7), and we need to add 1 to position 4 (from 5 to 6, then we need more? Wait: to make position 4 ≥ 6, we need at least 1, but after adding 1, it becomes 6, which is equal to 6. 

Wait: a[3] = 6 → after adding 1, becomes 7. a[4] = 5 → after adding 1, becomes 6. So 7 > 6 at the last pair. So we need a[4] ≥ 7. So we need to add at least 2 to position 4 (since 5+2=7).

So we need to add 2 to position 4. 

The amount to add to each position is determined by the chain of inequalities.

Actually, we need to ensure that for each position i, a[i] + (total added) ≤ a[i+1] + (total added to position i+1) for all i.

We can think of this as a system where we need to add enough to make the array non-decreasing.

Let me define b[i] = a[i] + (amount added to position i).

We want b[1] ≤ b[2] ≤ ... ≤ b[n].

For each position i where a[i] > a[i+1], we need b[i] ≤ b[i+1], so b[i+1] ≥ b[i], which means we need the amount added to position i+1 to be at least a[i] - a[i+1] + (amount added to i) - (amount added to i+1)? 

Actually: b[i] + add_i = a[i] + add_i ≤ a[i+1] + add_{i+1} = b[i+1]

So a[i] + add_i ≤ a[i+1] + add_{i+1}

Therefore: add_{i+1} - add_i ≥ a[i] - a[i+1]

So: add_{i+1} ≥ add_i + (a[i] - a[i+1])

This is a recurrence relation.

We need to find non-negative values for add_i such that for each i from 1 to n-1:
add_{i+1} ≥ add_i + (a[i] - a[i+1])

And add_1 ≥ 0.

The total amount added to each position is a sum of powers of 2 from seconds 1 to T.

For any amount we need to add to a position, we need at least the number of bits in its binary representation of seconds.

But we also have the constraint that add_{i+1} ≥ add_i + (a[i] - a[i+1]).

Let me define d_i = a[i] - a[i+1] for i from 1 to n-1.

If d_i > 0, then we need add_{i+1} ≥ add_i + d_i.

If d_i ≤ 0, no constraint (since a[i] ≤ a[i+1]).

So we start with add_1 = 0.

Then for each i from 1 to n-1:
- if d_i > 0, then add_{i+1} ≥ add_i + d_i
- if d_i ≤ 0, then no constraint (but we might still need to add to make things right?)

Actually, if d_i ≤ 0, then a[i] ≤ a[i+1], so no constraint from this pair.

But we might have constraints from previous positions.

So the recurrence is only for positive d_i.

Let me trace through example 1: [1,7,6,5]
d_1 = 7-1 = 6 > 0 → add_2 ≥ add_1 + 6 = 0 + 6 = 6
d_2 = 6-5 = 1 > 0 → add_3 ≥ add_2 + 1

But we have only 4 positions, so add_1, add_2, add_3, add_4.

Wait, the indices are 1,2,3,4 for positions.

d_1 = a[1] - a[2] = 1-7 = -6 ≤ 0 → no constraint
d_2 = a[2] - a[3] = 7-6 = 1 > 0 → add_3 ≥ add_2 + 1
d_3 = a[3] - a[4] = 6-5 = 1 > 0 → add_4 ≥ add_3 + 1

So add_3 ≥ add_2 + 1
add_4 ≥ add_3 + 1

We also have add_1 = 0 (we can start with that)

But we don't have a constraint on add_2 from d_1.

For each test case:

2. If already non-decreasing, return 0

Pairs:

i=1: a[1]=7, a[2]=6 → add[2] >= add[1] + (7-6) = add[1] + 1 → add[2] >= 1

- add[i+1] >= add[i] + (a[i]-a[i+1])
- a[i]-a[i+1] <= 0, so add[i+1] >= add[i] + negative number → add[i+1] >= add[i] - something, which is always true since add[i+1] >=0 and add[i] >=0
- So add remains all 0
max_add = 0 → return 0 → correct.

Example 3: [0, -4]
i=0: a[0]=0, a[1]=-4 → add[1] >= add[0] + (0 - (-4)) = add[0] + 4

max_add = 4

2^1-1=1 < 4 → T=2
2^2-1=3 < 4 → T=3
2^3-1=7 >=4 → T=3 → correct.

Perfect.

Edge case: when array is already non-decreasing, the constraints give add[i+1] >= add[i] + (a[i]-a[i+1]), and since a[i]-a[i+1] <= 0, this is always true (since add[i+1] >=0), so max_add=0.

So we can just compute the maximum of the add array after processing all constraints.

One more edge: if n=1, no pairs, so add array all 0, max_add=0, T=0.

Yes.

So final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # If already non-decreasing, we can return 0
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        print(0)
        continue
    
    # Initialize add array with zeros
    add = [0] * n
    
    # For each adjacent pair, apply the constraint
    for i in range(n-1):
        # We need: add[i+1] >= add[i] + (a[i] - a[i+1])
        required = add[i] + (a[i] - a[i+1])
        if required > add[i+1]:
            add[i+1] = required
    
    max_add = max(add)
    
    # Find minimum T such that 2^T - 1 >= max_add
    T = 0
    while (1 << T) - 1 < max_add:
        T += 1
    
    print(T)
```

Wait, in the loop for is_non_decreasing, I check if any a[i] > a[i+1]. But if a[i] > a[i+1], then it's not non-decreasing, so we return 0 only when no such pair exists.

Yes.

But what if the array is non-decreasing? Then we return 0 without processing the add array.

What if it's not? Then we compute the add array with constraints, then find T.

T: (1<<0)-1=0 <4 → T=1
(1<<1)-1=1 <4 → T=2
(1<<2)-1=3 <4 → T=3

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n - 1):
        if a[i] > a[i + 1]:
            non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if non_decreasing:
        print(0)
        continue
    
    # Add array to track minimum required additions
    add = [0] * n
    
    # Process each adjacent pair to enforce non-decreasing property
    for i in range(n - 1):
        required = add[i] + (a[i] - a[i + 1])
        if required > add[i + 1]:
            add[i + 1] = required
    
    max_add = max(add)
    
    # Find minimum T such that 2^T - 1 >= max_add
    T = 0
    while (1 << T) - 1 < max_add:
        T += 1
    
    print(T)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array `a` of length `n`
2. For each second `x`, we can add `2^(x-1)` to any subset of indices (including none)
3. We want to make the array non-decreasing as quickly as possible
4. We need to find the minimum number of seconds `T`

Key observations:
- The amounts we can add are powers of 2: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ...
- We can choose to add any subset of these powers to any positions
- Since powers of 2 are used, we can represent any non-negative integer as a sum of distinct powers of 2 (this is essentially binary representation)
- The operation at second `x` adds exactly `2^(x-1)` to selected positions

## Key Insight

The crucial insight is that we can think of this as being able to "fix" the array by adding powers of 2 to elements. Since we want the array to be non-decreasing, we need to ensure that for each position `i`, `a[i] <= a[i+1]`.

The operations allow us to add exactly powers of 2. Since powers of 2 are very specific, we need to think about when we might need to "fix" violations of the non-decreasing property.

Let me analyze when we need additional seconds:

1. If the array is already non-decreasing, answer is 0
2. Otherwise, we need to add powers of 2 to make it non-decreasing
3. The operations are additive and we can only add positive values

The key realization is that we can only add powers of 2, so if we need to make a difference of `d` between two adjacent elements, we need to determine if `d` can be represented as a sum of powers of 2 that we can access with the operations.

However, there's an important constraint: at second `x`, we add `2^(x-1)` to selected positions. So we can choose which subset of positions to add this value to.

Actually, let me think differently. Since we can add any combination of powers of 2 (as long as each power appears at most once), and we can apply operations sequentially, we need to determine the minimum number of seconds until we can achieve the non-decreasing condition.

Another key insight: we can think of the operations as allowing us to add any non-negative integer to any positions, but constrained by the fact that we add powers of 2 in a specific order.

But we also have to consider that deficits might be cumulative.

Actually, we can only increase, not decrease. So the only violations possible are when a[i] > a[i+1].

Actually, we can simulate the required additions greedily from left to right:

1. Initialize an array `add` of zeros with length n (how much we need to add to each position)

   - If a[i] > a[i+1], then we need to add at least (a[i] - a[i+1]) to position i+1
   - So add[i+1] += (a[i] - a[i+1])

Actually, we can use the fact that we can only add in powers of 2, but the key insight is that the total amount we need to add to any position is some non-negative integer, and since any non-negative integer has a binary representation, we just need the maximum deficit (the maximum amount we need to add to any single position across all deficit requirements) to have its highest bit covered by the powers of 2 available.

But the deficits are not just from direct pairs. When we add to a position, it fixes adjacent pairs, and we might need to add more to other positions due to cascading.

Actually, let me try to compute the minimum required additions in a greedy manner.

We can use a simple greedy from left to right:

- Start with the initial array
- Create a copy of the array and simulate the additions needed
- For each adjacent pair from left to right, if a[i] > a[i+1], then we must add at least (a[i] - a[i+1]) to a[i+1]
- After making these additions, repeat the process until no more violations exist

But this might not be efficient and might not be correct for the power of 2 constraint.

Wait, but in our operations, we can add any combination of powers of 2. After T seconds, we can add any integer value to any position (since any positive integer can be represented in binary with bits up to T-1).

So the only constraint is: can we represent the required amount of additions for each position using powers of 2 up to T-1?

For a position, if we need to add d, then we need T such that d can be written as sum of distinct powers of 2 from 2^0 to 2^(T-1), which is always possible as long as T > log2(d) (more precisely, as long as the highest bit of d is less than T).

So the condition is: for each position i, the total amount we need to add to i must have its highest bit <= (T-1).

So we need to compute the total amount needed for each position, then find the minimum T such that for all i, the highest bit of needed_add[i] is <= T-1.

But how to compute the needed_add array?

We can use a greedy algorithm that runs from left to right:

- Initialize an array `needed` of zeros
- For each i from 0 to n-2:
  - If a[i] + needed[i] > a[i+1] + needed[i+1], then we need to add more to position i+1
  - Specifically, we need to add at least (a[i] + needed[i] - (a[i+1] + needed[i+1])) to position i+1
  - So needed[i+1] += (a[i] + needed[i] - a[i+1] - needed[i+1])

But this is not correct because needed[i+1] is being modified in the middle of the loop.

Actually, we need to simulate it iteratively until stable.

Or we can do it in one pass from left to right, accumulating the required additions.

Here's a standard approach for "make non-decreasing by adding values":

We can compute the minimum additions needed from left to right:

1. Set current_value = a[0]
2. For i from 1 to n-1:
   - If a[i] < current_value, then we need to add (current_value - a[i]) to position i
   - Set current_value = a[i] + (amount_added_to_i)

But this is exactly what we need!

Let me trace this with the example [1,7,6,5]:

- i=1: a[1]=7, current_value=1, 7 > 1, so no need to add to position 1, current_value = 7
- i=2: a[2]=6, current_value=7, 6 < 7, so we need to add (7-6)=1 to position 2, so current_value = 6+1 = 7
- i=3: a[3]=5, current_value=7, 5 < 7, so we need to add (7-5)=2 to position 3, current_value = 5+2 = 7

So we need to add 1 to position 2 and 2 to position 3.

The amounts we need to add are [0, 0, 1, 2] (for positions 0,1,2,3)

The maximum amount needed is 2.

We need to represent 2 with powers of 2. 2 = 2^1, so we need powers up to 2^1, which means we need at least T such that 2^(T-1) >= 2.

So 2^(T-1) >= 2, so T-1 >= 1, so T >= 2.

Let's verify with T=2:
- We can add 1 and 2 at second 1 and 2
- We add 1 to position 2: second 1
- We add 2 to position 3: second 2
- So positions: [1,7,6+1=7,5+2=7] = [1,7,7,7] which is non-decreasing.

But in the example solution, they used T=2, and got [1,7,7,8].

In 0-based indexing: position 0:1, pos1:7, pos2:6, pos3:5.

Another example: [1,2,3,4,5] - already non-decreasing, so no additions needed. Maximum amount needed is 0. We need T such that we can represent 0. With T=0, we can add 0 to any positions, so answer is 0.

This matches the example output.

So the algorithm is:

1. If the array is already non-decreasing, return 0
2. Compute the required additions to each position using a greedy left-to-right approach:
   - Let `add` = [0] * n
   - Let `current_value = a[0]`
   - For i from 1 to n-1:
     - If a[i] < current_value:
       - amount_needed = current_value - a[i]
       - add[i] += amount_needed
       - current_value = a[i] + amount_needed
     - Else:
       - current_value = a[i]
3. Find the maximum value in the `add` array
4. If max_add == 0, return 0
5. Otherwise, find the smallest T such that the highest bit in max_add is less than T
   - That is, T > log2(max_add)
   - Actually, we need max_add <= (2^T - 1), and we want the minimum T such that this holds.
   - The maximum amount we can add with T seconds is 2^T - 1
   - So we need 2^T - 1 >= max_add
   - So T should be the smallest integer such that 2^T - 1 >= max_add
   - Which is equivalent to T >= log2(max_add + 1)

But since max_add is a non-negative integer:
- If max_add == 0, T = 0
- Otherwise, T = ceil(log2(max_add + 1))

For example:
- max_add = 1: log2(2) = 1, ceil(1) = 1, 2^1 - 1 = 1 >= 1 → T=1
  But wait, the amount 1 can be represented with second 1: 2^0 = 1, so T=1 is enough
- max_add = 2: log2(3) ≈ 1.58, ceil(1.58) = 2, 2^2 - 1 = 3 >= 2 → T=2
- max_add = 4: log2(5) ≈ 2.32, ceil(2.32) = 3, 2^3 - 1 = 7 >= 4 → T=3

But what about max_add=1? We need T such that the maximum power of 2 used is 2^0, so we need T >= 1 (since at second 1 we can use 2^0).

So T should be 1 for max_add=1.

But 2^T - 1 >= max_add:
- T=0: 0 >= 1? No
- T=1: 1 >= 1? Yes → T=1

So we need the smallest T such that 2^T - 1 >= max_add.

But in the example [0,-4], max_add=4, so:
- T=0: -1 >= 4? No
- T=1: 1 >= 4? No
- T=2: 3 >= 4? No
- T=3: 7 >= 4? Yes → T=3

Perfect.

So the algorithm:

For each test case:
1. Read n and array a
2. Check if already non-decreasing: for i in range(n-1): if a[i] > a[i+1]: break, else continue
   - If yes, print 0 and continue
3. Otherwise, compute the required additions using greedy from left to right
4. Find max_add in the additions array
5. Find smallest T such that (2^T - 1) >= max_add

But how to compute T efficiently?
- If max_add == 0, T = 0
- Else, T = 0
  while (2^T - 1) < max_add:
      T += 1
- Return T

But note: 2^T - 1 >= max_add
We can compute it directly with bit operations.

Actually, T = ceil(log2(max_add + 1))

But we can compute it safely in Python with bit_length:

In Python, for a positive integer x, `x.bit_length()` returns the number of bits needed, so for x=4, bit_length=3, and 2^(3-1)=4.

But we need T such that 2^T - 1 >= max_add.

For max_add = 1: 2^1 - 1 = 1 >= 1 → T=1
1.bit_length() = 1 → T = 1
For max_add=2: 2^2 - 1 = 3 >= 2 → T=2
2.bit_length() = 2 → T=2
For max_add=3: 2^2 - 1 = 3 >= 3 → T=2
3.bit_length() = 2 → T=2
For max_add=4: 2^3 - 1 = 7 >= 4 → T=3
4.bit_length() = 3 → T=3

So T = max_add.bit_length()

But what about max_add=0?
0.bit_length() returns 0, but we want T=0.

For max_add=0, we need T=0.
For max_add>0, we need T = max_add.bit_length()

Let me verify:
- max_add=1: bit_length=1 → T=1 → 2^1-1=1>=1 → correct
- max_add=2: bit_length=2 → T=2 → 3>=2 → correct
- max_add=3: bit_length=2 → 3>=3 → correct
- max_add=4: bit_length=3 → 7>=4 → correct
- max_add=5: bit_length=3 → 7>=5 → correct
- max_add=6: bit_length=3 → 7>=6 → correct
- max_add=7: bit_length=3 → 7>=7 → correct
- max_add=8: bit_length=4 → 15>=8 → correct

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:

1. If array is already non-decreasing: return 0
2. Else:
   - Compute required additions using greedy from left to right
   - Find max_add in the additions array
   - If max_add == 0: return 0
   - Else: return max_add.bit_length()

But what about the greedy computation?

Let me trace [1,7,6,5] again:
- i=1: a[1]=7, current_value=1 → 7>1, so current_value = 7
- i=2: a[2]=6, 6<7 → amount_needed = 7-6 = 1 → add[2] += 1, current_value = 6+1 = 7
- i=3: a[3]=5, 5<7 → amount_needed = 7-5 = 2 → add[3] += 2, current_value = 5+2 = 7
- add array: [0,0,1,2] → max_add = 2 → 2.bit_length() = 2 → answer = 2

Correct.

Another trace: [0,-4]
- i=1: a[1]=-4, current_value=0 → -4 < 0 → amount_needed = 0 - (-4) = 4 → add[1] += 4, current_value = -4 + 4 = 0
- add array: [0,4] → max_add = 4 → 4.bit_length() = 3 → answer = 3

Correct.

One more: [5,3,2] 
- i=1: a[1]=3, current_value=5 → 3<5 → amount_needed = 5-3=2 → add[1] +=2, current_value = 3+2=5
- i=2: a[2]=2, current_value=5 → 2<5 → amount_needed=3 → add[2] +=3, current_value=5
- add: [0,2,3] → max_add=3 → 3.bit_length()=2 → answer=2

Check: after adding 2 to pos1: [5,5,2], then add 3 to pos2: [5,5,5] → non-decreasing → T=2
Can we do it in T=1? With T=1, we can only add 1 to any position. We need to add at least 2 to pos1 and 3 to pos2, which requires at least 3, so 2^T-1 >= 3 → T>=2 → correct.

So the solution is:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        return 0
    
    # Greedy to compute required additions
    add = [0] * n
    current_val = a[0]
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        if a[i] < current_val:
            needed = current_val - a[i]
            add[i] += needed
            current_val = a[i] + needed
        else:
            current_val = a[i]
    
    max_add = max(add) if add else 0
    if max_add == 0:
        return 0
    else:
        return max_add.bit_length()

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, but what if the array has only one element? n=1, then no adjacent pairs, and it's always non-decreasing, so should return 0.

In our greedy loop, when n=1, we skip the loop for i in range(1,1), so add is [0], max_add=0, so return 0 → correct.

Also, edge case n=2: [3,1] 
- is_non_decreasing: 3>1 → false
- i=1: a[1]=1, current_val=3 → 1<3 → needed=2 → add[1]=2 → max_add=2 → 2.bit_length()=2 → return 2

With T=2: can we add 1 and 2 to position 1?
- At second 1: add 1 to position 1 → [3,2]
- At second 2: add 2 to position 1 → [3,4] → non-decreasing → works
- Can we do it in T=1? With T=1, can only add 1 to position 1 → [3,2] → still not non-decreasing → need T=2 → correct.

Perfect.

But one more thing: what about a case like [1,0,3]?
- i=1: a[1]=0, current_val=1 → 0<1 → needed=1 → add[1]=1 → current_val=0+1=1
- i=2: a[2]=3, current_val=1 → 3>1 → current_val=3
- add array: [0,1,0] → max_add=1 → 1.bit_length()=1 → return 1

Check: after adding 1 to position 1: [1,1,3] → non-decreasing → yes, T=1 is enough.

Yes.

So the final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        print(0)
        continue
    
    # Greedy to compute required additions
    add = [0] * n
    current_val = a[0]
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        if a[i] < current_val:
            needed = current_val - a[i]
            add[i] += needed
            current_val = a[i] + needed
        else:
            current_val = a[i]
    
    max_add = max(add)
    print(max_add.bit_length())
```

This should be the solution.

Let me run through the examples:

Example 1: [1,7,6,5] → not non-decreasing → add: after i=1: current_val=7; i=2: 6<7 → needed=1, current_val=7; i=3: 5<7 → needed=2, current_val=7 → add=[0,0,1,2] → max_add=2 → 2.bit_length()=2 → output 2

Example 2: [1,2,3,4,5] → non-decreasing → output 0

Example 3: [0,-4] → not non-decreasing → i=1: -4<0 → needed=4, current_val=0 → add=[0,4] → max_add=4 → 4.bit_length()=3 → output 3

Perfect.

Note: There might be a case where we need to add more than once to a position due to cascading, but in our greedy method, we only add when we encounter a violation, and we process from left to right, so it should be correct.

Actually, our greedy method might not capture all violations? Let me think of a counterexample.

Suppose [4, 1, 5]
- Initially: 4 > 1 → violation at (0,1)
- In the greedy:
  - i=1: a[1]=1 < current_val=4 → needed=3 → add[1] +=3, current_val = 1+3=4
  - i=2: a[2]=5 >= current_val=4 → current_val=5
- So add array: [0,3,0] → max_add=3 → 3.bit_length()=2 → answer=2

After adding 3 to position 1: [4,4,5] → non-decreasing → yes, T=2.

Can we do it with T=1? Only add 1 to any position. Add 1 to position 1: [4,2,5] → 4>2 → still not good. So need at least T=2 because we need to add 3 to position 1, and 3 requires 2^1 + 2^0 = 3 (seconds 1 and 2). So T=2 is minimal.

Yes.

Another example: [3, 1, 2]
- i=1: 1<3 → needed=2 → position 1 gets +2 → current_val=3
- i=2: 2<3 → needed=1 → position 2 gets +1 → current_val=3
- add = [0,2,1] → max_add=2 → 2.bit_length()=2 → T=2

After: [3,3,3] → good.

With T=1: can only add 1. If add 1 to pos1: [3,2,2] → 3>2 → bad. If add 1 to pos2: [3,1,3] → 1<3 → violation at (1,2) is gone but (0,1) still bad. If add to both: [3,2,3] → still 3>2 → bad. So T=1 not enough. T=2 is needed → correct.

So the solution is correct.

Final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        print(0)
        continue
    
    # Greedy to compute required additions
    add = [0] * n
    current_val = a[0]
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        if a[i] < current_val:
            needed = current_val - a[i]
            add[i] += needed
            current_val = a[i] + needed
        else:
            current_val = a[i]
    
    max_add = max(add)
    print(max_add.bit_length())
```

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Check if already non-decreasing
    is_non_decreasing = True
    for i in range(n-1):
        if a[i] > a[i+1]:
            is_non_decreasing = False
            break
    
    if is_non_decreasing:
        print(0)
        continue
    
    # Greedy to compute required additions
    add = [0] * n
    current_val = a[0]
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        if a[i] < current_val:
            needed = current_val - a[i]
            add[i] += needed
            current_val = a[i] + needed
        else:
            current_val = a[i]
    
    max_add = max(add)
    print(max_add.bit_length())

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You have an array $a$ of length $n$. For every positive integer $x$ you are going to perform the following operation during the $x$-th second:

  Select some distinct indices $i_{1}, i_{2}, \ldots, i_{k}$ which are between $1$ and $n$ inclusive, and add $2^{x-1}$ to each corresponding position of $a$. Formally, $a_{i_{j}} := a_{i_{j}} + 2^{x-1}$ for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, k$. Note that you are allowed to not select any indices at all. 

You have to make $a$ nondecreasing as fast as possible. Find the smallest number $T$ such that you can make the array nondecreasing after at most $T$ seconds.

Array $a$ is nondecreasing if and only if $a_{1} \le a_{2} \le \ldots \le a_{n}$.

You have to answer $t$ independent test cases.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 10^{4}$) — the number of test cases.

The first line of each test case contains single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10^{5}$) — the length of array $a$. It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases in the input does not exceed $10^{5}$.

The second line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}$ ($-10^{9} \le a_{i} \le 10^{9}$).


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the minimum number of seconds in which you can make $a$ nondecreasing.


-----Example-----
Input
3
4
1 7 6 5
5
1 2 3 4 5
2
0 -4

Output
2
0
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, if you select indices $3, 4$ at the $1$-st second and $4$ at the $2$-nd second, then $a$ will become $[1, 7, 7, 8]$. There are some other possible ways to make $a$ nondecreasing in $2$ seconds, but you can't do it faster.

In the second test case, $a$ is already nondecreasing, so answer is $0$.

In the third test case, if you do nothing at first $2$ seconds and select index $2$ at the $3$-rd second, $a$ will become $[0, 0]$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). 1. We have an array `a` of length `n`

- The amount we add in second `x` is `2^(x-1)`, which is a power of 2
- Since we can choose any subset of indices, we have fine control over which elements get incremented
- Powers of 2 have the property that any positive integer can be represented as a sum of distinct powers of 2 (binary representation)
- The goal is to make the array non-decreasing

Let me define s[i] = sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k] for i>=2, s[1]=0
Then x[i] >= s[i] if we set x[1]=0, and x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i]

But if we set x[i] = max(0, s[i]), then x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i] might not hold.

For example, if d[1] = -2, then s[2] = -2, so x[2] = 0
Then x[3] >= x[2] + d[2] = 0 + d[2]

So if d[2] = -3, then x[3] >= -3, which is always true since x[3] >= 0.

So the only constraint is that x[i] >= max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k]) for each i.

And since we can choose x[i] to be larger, we can always make it work.

The minimal values are:
x[1] = 0
x[i] = max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k]) for i from 2 to n

With this choice, we have x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i] because:
x[i+1] >= max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i} d[k]) = max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k] + d[i]) 
x[i] = max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k])

And max(0, A) + d[i] >= max(0, A + d[i]) only if d[i] >=0, but in general, it's not true.

For example, if A=5, d[i]=-3, then max(0,5)+(-3)=5-3=2, max(0,5-3)=max(0,2)=2, which is >=2.
If A=5, d[i]=-6, then 5-6=-1, max(0,-1)=0, and max(0,5-6)=0, 0>=0.
If A=-3, d[i]=2, then max(0,-3)+2=0+2=2, max(0,-3+2)=max(0,-1)=0, and 2 >= 0, true.

In all cases, max(0, A) + d[i] >= max(0, A + d[i])? Let's check:
- If A >=0 and d[i] >=0: left = A + d[i], right = A + d[i], equal
- If A >=0 and d[i] <0: left = A + d[i], right = max(0, A+d[i]) = A+d[i] if A+d[i] >=0, or 0 if A+d[i] <0. Since A+d[i] <0, right =0, left = A+d[i] <0, so left <0, right=0, so left < right, not >=
This is the problem.

So when A >=0 and d[i] <0 and A+d[i] <0, we have left = A+d[i] <0, right =0, so left < right, so the inequality x[i+1] >= x[i] + d[i] is not satisfied by x[i+1] = max(0, A+d[i]) =0.

Therefore, the minimal solution is not simply max(0, sum_{k=1}^{i-1} d[k]).

I think I need to abandon this approach.

Back to the example solutions:

In example 1: [1,7,6,5]
We need to add to position 2 to fix a[1]>a[2] (7>6) -> add at least 1
We need to add to position 3 to fix a[2]>a[3] (7>6) -> after adding, a[2] becomes 8, so a[2]=8 > a[3]=6, so we need to add at least 2 to position 3 to make a[3]=8
We need to add to position 4 to fix a[3]>a[4] (6>5) -> after adding to position 3, a[3]=8, so a[3]=8 > a[4]=5, so we need to add at least 3 to position 4.

So the amount to add to position 2: 1
 to position 3: 2
 to position 4: 3

The maximum amount is 3, and 3 in binary has bits 0 and 1, so T=2 (since 2^0 and 2^1).

In example 3: [0,-4]
- a[1]=0, a[2]=-4
- need to add to position 2 to make it >=0, so at least 4
- 4 in binary: 100, so T=3 (2^2)

In example 2: [1,2,3,4,5] - already non-decreasing, so no additions needed, so T=0.

So the algorithm might be:
1. Start with a copy of a
2. For each position i from 1 to n-1, if a[i] > a[i+1], then we need to add to position i+1
3. But we need to do it in a way that respects the chain
4. Actually, we can simulate from left to right, and at each step, if a[i] > a[i+1], then we must add at least (a[i] - a[i+1]) to position i+1
5. But after that, a[i+1] increases, so we need to check again
6. This could be infinite or we might need to do it until convergence

But in the examples, it's not linear.

In example 1: [1,7,6,5]
i=1: a[1]=1, a[2]=7, 1<=7, ok
i=2: a[2]=7, a[3]=6, 7>6, so need to add at least (7-6)=1 to position 3
Now a becomes [1,7,7,5]
i=3: a[3]=7, a[4]=5, 7>5, need to add (7-5)=2 to position 4
Now a becomes [1,7,7,7]
i=2: a[2]=7, a[3]=7, ok
i=3: a[3]=7, a[4]=7, ok

So we need to add 1 to position 3 and 2 to position 4.

Max amount = 2, which requires T=2 (2^1 = 2, so seconds 1 and 2, 2^0 and 2^1, so we need to cover up to 2^1)

2 in binary has bit at 1, so T=2 (since 2^0 and 2^1 are used, so T must be at least 2)

In example 3: [0,-4]
i=1: a[1]=0, a[2]=-4, 0 > -4, so need to add (0 - (-4)) = 4 to position 2
Now a[2] = -4 + 4 = 0
Now a = [0,0] - non-decreasing
So we need to add 4 to position 2
4 in binary: 100, so T=3

This matches.

So the algorithm is:
- Keep a array
- While there is a violation (a[i] > a[i+1] for some i), do the following:
  - Find the leftmost i such that a[i] > a[i+1]
  - Add (a[i] - a[i+1]) to a[i+1]
  - This might create new violations, so continue
- After this process, we have a non-decreasing array
- Then, find the maximum amount that was added to any position
- Let max_add = the maximum over all positions of the amount added to that position
- Then, the answer is the number of bits in max_add, if max_add > 0, else 0
- Number of bits in number k (k>0) is floor(log2(k)) + 1

For example 1: added to pos3:1, to pos4:2, max_add=2, floor(log2(2)) +1 = 1+1 =2
Example 3: added to pos2:4, max_add=4, floor(log2(4)) +1 = 2+1 =3
Example 2: no additions, max_add=0, answer=0

But is this the minimal T? In example 1, can we do it in 1 second? 
With 1 second, we can only add 1 to any positions. 
After adding 1 to position 3: [1,7,7,5] -> still 7>5
After adding 1 to position 4: [1,7,6,6] -> 7>6
After adding 1 to both: [1,7,7,6] -> 7>6
So not possible. So T=2 is correct.

Is this algorithm correct? Let me test with a simple example: [3,1]
- i=1: a[1]=3> a[2]=1, so add (3-1)=2 to position 2
- new array: [3,3] - non-decreasing
- max_add = 2
- T = floor(log2(2)) +1 = 1+1 =2

Can we do it in 1 second? In second 1, we can add 1 to any positions. If we add to position 2, becomes [3,2], still 3>2. So not non-decreasing. So T=2 is correct.

Another example: [5,3,1]

- max_add = max(2,4) =4

Can we do it in 2 seconds? With seconds 1 and 2: can add 1 and 2 to any positions.
We need to add at least 2 to position 2 and at least 4 to position 3.
4 requires 2^2, which is in second 3 (2^(3-1)=2^2=4), so we need second 3. So T=3 is correct.

In our algorithm, we are only adding to position i+1 when a[i] > a[i+1], and we do it in a while loop until no violations.

But is this the only way? What if we add to both positions? In the above, we only add to position 2 and then to position 3. But could we add to position 3 in the first step? If we add to position 3 in second 1, it becomes [5,3,2], then still 5>3 and 3>2, so we need to add more. If we add to position 2 in second 1: [5,4,1], then still violations. If we add to both in second 1: [5,4,2], still 5>4 and 4>2.
