   a. leftmost = first i where not covered[i]
   b. best_gain = -1
   c. best_occurrence = None
   d. For each valid occurrence (si, start, end):
        - if this occurrence covers leftmost (i.e., leftmost >= start and leftmost <= end)
        - calculate the number of uncovered positions in [start, end]
        - if this number > best_gain, best_gain = that number, best_occurrence = (si, start)
   e. If best_gain == -1: return -1  (impossible)
   f. Add (si+1, start+1) to steps (convert to 1-indexed)
   g. Mark all positions from start to end as covered
6. Output the steps

This should work.

Let me test with the first example:
t = "bababa", strings = ["ba", "aba"]

Valid occurrences:
"ba" (index 0): 
- start 0: t[0:2]="ba" -> matches -> covers 0,1
- start 1: t[1:3]="ab" -> "ab" vs "ba" -> no
- start 3: t[3:5]="ab" -> "ab" vs "ba" -> no
- start 4: t[4:6]="ba" -> matches -> covers 4,5
- So only (0,0), (0,4)

"aba" (index 1):
- start 1: t[1:4]="aba" -> matches -> covers 1,2,3
- start 3: t[3:6]="aba" -> matches -> covers 3,4,5
- So (1,1), (1,3)

Now:
covered = [f,f,f,f,f,f]
leftmost = 0
Check occurrences covering 0:
- "ba" at 0: covers positions 0,1; uncovered in [0,1]: 0 is uncovered, 1 is uncovered -> 2 uncovered positions
- "aba" at 1: covers 1,2,3; 0 not covered -> skip
- So only "ba" at 0
- Pick it: steps = [(0+1, 0+1) = (1,1)]
- Mark positions 0,1 as covered

covered = [t,t,f,f,f,f]

leftmost = 2
Check occurrences covering 2:
- "ba" at 0: covers 0,1 -> doesn't cover 2
- "ba" at 4: covers 4,5 -> doesn't cover 2
- "aba" at 1: covers 1,2,3 -> covers 2
  - in [1,3]: covered positions: 1 (covered), 2 (uncovered), 3 (uncovered) -> uncovered: 2,3 -> 2 positions
- "aba" at 3: covers 3,4,5 -> covers 3,4,5; 2 not covered -> no
- So only "aba" at 1 covers position 2
- Pick it: steps = [(1,1), (2,2)]  (string 2 at position 2 in 1-indexed)
- Mark positions 1,2,3 as covered
- Now covered: 0,1,2,3

leftmost = 4
Check occurrences covering 4:
- "ba" at 4: covers 4,5; uncovered in [4,5]: 4,5 both uncovered -> 2 positions
- "aba" at 3: covers 3,4,5; uncovered: 4,5 -> 2 positions
- Both have 2 uncovered positions
- Pick one, say "ba" at 4: steps = [(1,1), (2,2), (1,5)] (string 1 at position 5)
- Mark positions 4,5 as covered

All covered. Steps: 3

But in our earlier analysis, we said "ba" at 4 covers 4,5 and position 4 is uncovered, but when we check, after covering 0,1,2,3, position 4 is uncovered.

Yes.

So steps: (1,1), (2,2), (1,5)

In 1-indexed: string 1 at position 1, string 2 at position 2, string 1 at position 5.

But the expected output is:
3
2 2
1 1
2 4

Which is different but valid.

So any valid solution with 3 steps is acceptable.

So the algorithm works.

I'll implement it.

Note: when we have multiple occurrences with the same gain, we can pick any.

Let me write the code.

Edge: if no occurrence covers leftmost, return -1.

Also, when we compute covered positions, we only count positions that are uncovered before.

We only need to check if the occurrence starts at or before leftmost and ends at or after leftmost.

In code:

```python
def find_occurrences(t, strings):
    n = len(strings)
    occurrences = []  # (string_index, start_pos)
    for i, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        for start in range(len(t) - len_s + 1):
            if t[start:start+len_s] == s:
                occurrences.append((i, start))
    return occurrences
```

Then in the greedy loop.

But note: when we pick an occurrence, we mark all positions from start to start+len_s-1 as covered.

Now let me code the entire solution.

We'll read q test cases.

```python
q = int(input())
for _ in range(q):
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find all valid occurrences
    occurrences = []
    for i, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        for start in range(len(t) - len_s + 1):
            if t[start:start+len_s] == s:
                occurrences.append((i, start))
    
    # If no occurrences at all, impossible
    if not occurrences:
        print(-1)
        continue
        
    covered = [False] * len(t)
    steps = []
    
    while True:
        # Find leftmost uncovered position
        leftmost = -1
        for i in range(len(t)):
            if not covered[i]:
                leftmost = i
                break
        if leftmost == -1:
            break  # all covered
            
        best_gain = -1
        best_occurrence = None
        
        # Check all valid occurrences
        for si, start in occurrences:
            end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
            # Check if this occurrence covers leftmost
            if start <= leftmost <= end:
                # Count how many uncovered positions in [start, end]
                gain = 0
                for pos in range(start, end + 1):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        gain += 1
                if gain > best_gain:
                    best_gain = gain
                    best_occurrence = (si, start)
        
        if best_gain == -1:
            print(-1)
            break
            
        # Add the step
        steps.append((best_occurrence[0] + 1, best_occurrence[1] + 1))
        
        # Mark covered positions from start to end
        si, start = best_occurrence
        end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
    
    # If we exited the loop normally (all covered), output steps
    if leftmost == -1:  # all positions covered
        print(len(steps))
        for step in steps:
            print(step[0], step[1])
    else:
        print(-1)
```

But wait, in the while loop, when we break because all covered, we have leftmost == -1, and we print steps.

But if we break because best_gain == -1 (impossible), we print -1 and break the while loop, then we don't output steps.

So the structure is:

```python
    covered = [False] * len(t)
    steps = []
    
    while True:
        # Find leftmost uncovered position
        leftmost = -1
        for i in range(len(t)):
            if not covered[i]:
                leftmost = i
                break
        if leftmost == -1:
            break  # all covered
            
        best_gain = -1
        best_occurrence = None
        
        for si, start in occurrences:
            end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
            if start <= leftmost <= end:
                gain = 0
                for pos in range(start, end + 1):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        gain += 1
                if gain > best_gain:
                    best_gain = gain
                    best_occurrence = (si, start)
        
        if best_gain == -1:
            print(-1)
            break
            
        steps.append((best_occurrence[0] + 1, best_occurrence[1] + 1))
        si, start = best_occurrence
        end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
    
    # If we broke from the while loop due to all covered, output steps
    # If we broke due to impossible, we already printed -1
    if leftmost == -1:  # all covered
        print(len(steps))
        for step in steps:
            print(step[0], step[1])
```

But the "leftmost == -1" check at the start of the loop is not updated in the loop body when we cover positions.

Actually, when we break the loop when leftmost == -1, that happens after we've done a full iteration and found no uncovered positions.

So after the loop, we check if leftmost was -1 at the start of the loop.

But in the code, we set leftmost at the start of the loop, and if it's -1, we break.

So after the loop, we don't know if we broke due to impossible or due to all covered.

We need to check if we broke due to impossible.

We can do:

```python
    covered = [False] * len(t)
    steps = []
    found_impossible = False
    
    while True:
        # Find leftmost uncovered position
        leftmost = -1
        for i in range(len(t)):
            if not covered[i]:
                leftmost = i
                break
        if leftmost == -1:
            break  # all covered
            
        best_gain = -1
        best_occurrence = None
        
        for si, start in occurrences:
            end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
            if start <= leftmost <= end:
                gain = 0
                for pos in range(start, end + 1):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        gain += 1
                if gain > best_gain:
                    best_gain = gain
                    best_occurrence = (si, start)
        
        if best_gain == -1:
            found_impossible = True
            break
            
        steps.append((best_occurrence[0] + 1, best_occurrence[1] + 1))
        si, start = best_occurrence
        end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
    
    if found_impossible:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(len(steps))
        for step in steps:
            print(step[0], step[1])
```

This should work.

- start1: "a b a" -> "aba" != "bac"
- no

"acab": len=4, t="caba" (length 4)
- start0: "c a b a" -> "caba" != "acab"
- no

So occurrences list is empty.

Then in the while loop:
- leftmost = 0 (since position 0 is uncovered)
- then we loop over occurrences: for si, start in empty list, so no iteration
- best_gain remains -1
- so we set found_impossible = True
- break
- then print -1

- leftmost = 0
- check: si=0, start=0, end=0
- 0<=0<=0 -> yes
- gain: for pos in [0,0]: if not covered[0] -> uncovered, so gain=1
- best_gain=1, best_occurrence=(0,0)
- steps.append((1,1))
- mark covered[0] = True
- then leftmost = -1, break the while loop
- print 1
- print "1 1"

I'll write the final code.

Note: in the string index, we use the input order: first string is index 0, so when we have (i, start), we use i.

We output (i+1, start+1)

Let me code accordingly.

```python
q = int(input())
for _ in range(q):
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    # Find all valid occurrences: (string_index, start_position_0indexed)
    occurrences = []
    for i, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        for start in range(len(t) - len_s + 1):
            if t[start:start+len_s] == s:
                occurrences.append((i, start))
    
    # If no occurrences, impossible
    covered = [False] * len(t)
    steps = []
    found_impossible = False
    
    while True:
        # Find the leftmost uncovered position
        leftmost = -1
        for i in range(len(t)):
            if not covered[i]:
                leftmost = i
                break
        if leftmost == -1:
            # All positions are covered
            break
            
        best_gain = -1
        best_occurrence = None
        
        # Check all possible occurrences that cover leftmost
        for si, start in occurrences:
            end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
            # Check if this occurrence covers leftmost
            if start <= leftmost <= end:
                # Count how many uncovered positions in [start, end]
                gain = 0
                for pos in range(start, end + 1):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        gain += 1
                if gain > best_gain:
                    best_gain = gain
                    best_occurrence = (si, start)
        
        # If no occurrence covers leftmost, impossible
        if best_gain == -1:
            found_impossible = True
            break
            
        # Add this step
        step_string_idx = best_occurrence[0] + 1
        step_pos = best_occurrence[1] + 1
        steps.append((step_string_idx, step_pos))
        
        # Mark the entire occurrence as covered
        si, start = best_occurrence
        end = start + len(strings[si]) - 1
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
    
    if found_impossible:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(len(steps))
        for step in steps:
            print(step[0], step[1])
```

This should be the solution.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given some text $t$ and a set of $n$ strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_n$.

In one step, you can choose any occurrence of any string $s_i$ in the text $t$ and color the corresponding characters of the text in red. For example, if $t={bababa}$ and $s_1={ba}$, $s_2={aba}$, you can get $t={{ba}}{baba}$, $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$ or $t={bab}{{aba}}$ in one step.

You want to color all the letters of the text $t$ in red. When you color a letter in red again, it stays red.

In the example above, three steps are enough:

Let's color $t[2 \dots 4]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[1 \dots 2]=s_1={ba}$ in red, we get $t={{baba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[4 \dots 6]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={{bababa}}$.

Each string $s_i$ can be applied any number of times (or not at all). Occurrences for coloring can intersect arbitrarily.

Determine the minimum number of steps needed to color all letters $t$ in red and how to do it. If it is impossible to color all letters of the text $t$ in red, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains an integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 100$) —the number of test cases in the test.

The descriptions of the test cases follow.

The first line of each test case contains the text $t$ ($1 \le |t| \le 100$), consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|t|$ is the length of the text $t$.

The second line of each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10$) — the number of strings in the set.

This is followed by $n$ lines, each containing a string $s_i$ ($1 \le |s_i| \le 10$) consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|s_i|$ — the length of string $s_i$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the answer on a separate line.

If it is impossible to color all the letters of the text in red, print a single line containing the number -1.

Otherwise, on the first line, print the number $m$ — the minimum number of steps it will take to turn all the letters $t$ red.

Then in the next $m$ lines print pairs of indices: $w_j$ and $p_j$ ($1 \le j \le m$), which denote that the string with index $w_j$ was used as a substring to cover the occurrences starting in the text $t$ from position $p_j$. The pairs can be output in any order.

If there are several answers, output any of them.

Actually, this is the minimum covering by intervals (each interval corresponds to an occurrence of a string), and each occurrence is a contiguous interval.

So the problem reduces to: given a set of intervals (each occurrence of a string), find the minimum number of intervals that cover all points in [0, len(t)-1].

This is the classic "minimum interval cover" or "set cover with intervals" where the items are points and the sets are intervals.

This is NP-hard in general, but in our case, the universe is a one-dimensional interval [0, L-1] with L ≤ 100, and the number of intervals is at most 1000.

There is a greedy algorithm that for interval covering (covering points) is not optimal, but for covering a line with intervals, there is a known greedy: at each step, pick the interval that covers the leftmost uncovered point and has the largest right endpoint.

This is the standard "activity selection" style, but for covering points, it's different.

Actually, the standard algorithm for minimum interval cover (to cover all points) is: while there are uncovered points, pick the interval that covers the leftmost uncovered point and has the largest right endpoint.

This is known to be optimal for covering points with intervals, and it's called the "greedy covering algorithm".

Let me verify with the example:
- Positions: 0,1,2,3,4,5
- Initially, leftmost uncovered is 0.
- Intervals covering 0: 
  - "ba" at 0: covers [0,1] -> right endpoint 1
  - "aba" at 1: covers [1,3] -> doesn't cover 0
  - others: don't cover 0
  - so only "ba" at 0: right endpoint 1
- Pick this: covers [0,1]
- Now uncovered: 2,3,4,5
- leftmost uncovered: 2
- intervals covering 2:
  - "ba" at 2: covers [2,3] -> right endpoint 3
  - "aba" at 1: covers [1,3] -> doesn't cover 2? 2>=1 and 2<3 -> yes, covers 2
  - "aba" at 3: covers [3,5] -> covers 3,4,5, does it cover 2? 2<3 -> no
  - so "ba" at 2: right endpoint 3
  - "aba" at 1: right endpoint 3
  - both have right endpoint 3, so we can pick either
- Pick "ba" at 2: covers [2,3]
- uncovered: 4,5
- leftmost uncovered: 4
- intervals covering 4:
  - "ba" at 4: covers [4,5] -> right endpoint 5
  - "aba" at 3: covers [3,5] -> covers 4,5 -> right endpoint 5
  - both have right endpoint 5
- pick one, say "ba" at 4: covers [4,5]
- total steps: 3, which is optimal.

This works.

So the greedy algorithm for covering points: 
- While there are uncovered positions:
  - Find the leftmost uncovered position, call it L
  - Among all intervals that cover L, find the one with the largest right endpoint (j + len(s_i) - 1)
  - Add that interval to the solution
  - Mark all positions in that interval as covered

This is known to be optimal for the problem of covering points with intervals.

Why? Because it's a standard result that this greedy gives the optimal minimum number of intervals.

Therefore, I will use this greedy algorithm.

Steps:
1. Generate all possible occurrences of each string (store as (string_index, start_position, end_position = start + len(s_i)-1))
2. For each occurrence, it covers [start, end]
3. While there are uncovered positions:
   - Find the leftmost uncovered position L
   - Among all occurrences that cover L, find the one with the largest end_position
   - Add that occurrence to solution
   - Mark all positions from start to end as covered
4. Output the result in 1-indexed, with (string_index+1, start_position+1)

Also, before that, verify that every position is coverable by at least one occurrence.

Implementation:

Note: if there are multiple occurrences with the same end position, we can pick any.

Edge: if no occurrence covers the leftmost uncovered position, then it's impossible, but we already checked that in step 1.

In step 1, when we said "for each position in t, is there any occurrence covering it", we did that for being theoretically coverable. But this greedy will only work if at the leftmost uncovered point, there is at least one occurrence that covers it.

So we are safe.

  - "bac" at 1: would start at 1, length 3: t[1:4]="aba" != "bac"
  - "acab" at 0: length 4, t[0:4]="caba" != "acab"
  - "acab" at 1: out of bounds
  - so no occurrence covers position 0 -> return -1

In our greedy algorithm, when we are at position 0, we look for occurrences covering position 0.

- "c" at 0: "c" vs "b" -> no
- "b" at 0: "b" vs "b" -> yes, and it covers [0,0], end_pos=0

  - so (1,3) -> becomes (1,4)

Final code structure:

For each test case:
  t = input()
  n = int(input())
  strings = []
  for i in range(n):
      strings.append(input())

  # Step 1: Check if every position in t can be covered by some occurrence of some string
  len_t = len(t)
  can_cover = [False] * len_t
  occurrences = []  # list of (string_index, start_position, end_position)
  for i in range(n):
      s = strings[i]
      len_s = len(s)
      for j in range(len_t - len_s + 1):
          if t[j:j+len_s] == s:
              end_pos = j + len_s - 1
              occurrences.append((i, j, end_pos))
              # Also mark that position j to end_pos can be covered by this occurrence
              for k in range(j, end_pos+1):
                  can_cover[k] = True

  # If any position is not covered, output -1
  if not all(can_cover):
      print(-1)
  else:
      # Use greedy covering by intervals
      covered = [False] * len_t
      steps = []
      
      while not all(covered):
          # Find the leftmost uncovered position
          leftmost = -1
          for i in range(len_t):
              if not covered[i]:
                  leftmost = i
                  break
          
          # Find the occurrence (i, j, end) that covers leftmost and has the largest end_pos
          best_end = -1
          best_occur = None
          
          for (string_idx, start, end) in occurrences:
              if start <= leftmost <= end:
                  if end > best_end:
                      best_end = end
                      best_occur = (string_idx, start, end)
          
          # If no occurrence covers leftmost, should not happen due to can_cover
          if best_occur is None:
              # This should not happen if can_cover is true
              print(-1)
              break
              
          # Add this occurrence to steps
          steps.append(best_occur)
          
          # Mark all positions from start to end as covered
          for pos in range(start, end+1):
              covered[pos] = True
              
      # Output
      print(len(steps))
      for (string_idx, start, end) in steps:
          print(string_idx+1, start+1)  # 1-indexed

But wait, the greedy might not be optimal in terms of minimum steps? In the earlier example with "bababa", we saw that it gave 3 steps, which is optimal.

And in the other examples, it seems to work.

But is it proven to be optimal for covering points with intervals?

Yes, the greedy algorithm of: at each step, cover the leftmost uncovered point with an interval that has the largest right endpoint, is known to give the optimal solution for the minimum number of intervals needed to cover a set of points on a line.

This is a standard result.

Therefore, I will use this.

  - "forces" has length 6, from 0: "codeforc" -> not

  - from 2: "defor" -> only 5 chars
  - from 3: "eforc" -> only 5 chars

  - so no occurrence of "forces"

  - "efo" at 3: covers 3,4,5 -> covers 4 -> end=5
  - "forces" at 4: covers 4,5,6,7,8,9 -> end=9
  - so best is "forces" at 4: end=9
- pick it: covers [4,9]
- all positions covered

So steps: 
- (1,0) -> string index 1 (since "code" is index 1), start 0 -> 1-indexed: (2,1)
- (3,4) -> string index 3 ("forces"), start 4 -> 1-indexed: (4,5)

(2,1): string_index 1 -> "code", start 0 -> (2,1)

Let me write the final code.

Note: there might be multiple occurrences that cover the leftmost uncovered point and have the same end. We pick the first one with the largest end (or any one).

We can break ties arbitrarily.

Now, code accordingly.

Edge: if no occurrence covers leftmost, we break and print -1, but this should not happen due to the can_cover check.

Let's code.

Note: we must read q test cases.

Final code:

```python
def solve():
    q = int(input().strip())
    for _ in range(q):
        t = input().strip()
        n = int(input().strip())
        strings = []
        for _ in range(n):
            strings.append(input().strip())
        
        len_t = len(t)
        # Check if every position can be covered by some occurrence of some string
        can_cover = [False] * len_t
        occurrences = []  # (string_index, start, end)
        
        for i in range(n):
            s = strings[i]
            len_s = len(s)
            for j in range(len_t - len_s + 1):
                if t[j:j + len_s] == s:
                    end_pos = j + len_s - 1
                    occurrences.append((i, j, end_pos))
                    for k in range(j, end_pos + 1):
                        can_cover[k] = True
        
        # If any position is not covered, output -1
        if not all(can_cover):
            print(-1)
        else:
            covered = [False] * len_t
            steps = []
            
            while not all(covered):
                # Find the leftmost uncovered position
                leftmost = -1
                for i in range(len_t):
                    if not covered[i]:
                        leftmost = i
                        break
                
                best_end = -1
                best_occur = None
                for (string_idx, start, end) in occurrences:
                    if start <= leftmost <= end:
                        if end > best_end:
                            best_end = end
                            best_occur = (string_idx, start, end)
                
                if best_occur is None:
                    # Should not happen, but for safety
                    print(-1)
                    break
                    
                steps.append(best_occur)
                # Mark positions from start to end as covered
                for pos in range(best_occur[1], best_occur[2] + 1):
                    covered[pos] = True
                    
            # Output the answer
            print(len(steps))
            for (string_idx, start, end) in steps:
                print(string_idx + 1, start + 1)

solve()
```

Let me run through the provided examples to be sure.

Example 1: t="bababa", n=2, strings=["ba","aba"]
- should output 3 steps with the given pairs.

Our algorithm:
- can_cover: all positions covered
- occurrences:
  - "ba": at 0: covers [0,1], at 2: [2,3], at 4: [4,5]
  - "aba": at 1: [1,3], at 3: [3,5]
- covered = [f,f,f,f,f,f]
- leftmost = 0
- intervals covering 0: 
  - "ba" at 0: covers 0,1 -> end=1
  - others: "aba" at 1: starts at 1, doesn't cover 0
  - so only one: end=1
- pick (0,0,1) -> covers [0,1]
- covered: [t,t,f,f,f,f]
- leftmost=2
- intervals covering 2:
  - "ba" at 2: covers [2,3] -> end=3
  - "aba" at 1: covers [1,3] -> covers 2 (1<=2<=3) -> end=3
  - both have end=3, so we pick one, say first: (0,2,3) or (1,1,3)
- if we pick (0,2,3): covers [2,3] -> covered: [t,t,t,t,f,f]
- leftmost=4
- intervals covering 4:
  - "ba" at 4: [4,5] -> end=5
  - "aba" at 3: [3,5] -> covers 4 -> end=5
  - pick one, say (0,4,5): covers [4,5]
- steps: (0,0,1), (0,2,3), (0,4,5) -> in 1-indexed: (1,1), (1,3), (1,5)
- but expected: 
  3
  2 2
  1 1
  2 4

Which is (2,2), (1,1), (2,4) -> in 0-indexed: (1,1), (0,0), (1,3)

Our solution has different steps but same number of steps and covers all.

The problem says "output any of them", so it's valid.

But in the example, they used (1,1) which is "ba" at 0, which is our first step, and (2,2) which is "aba" at 1 (start=1), which covers [1,3] -> would cover positions 1,2,3.

In our algorithm, after covering [0,1], leftmost uncovered is 2.
- "ba" at 2: covers [2,3] -> end=3
- "aba" at 1: covers [1,3] -> covers 2,3 -> end=3
- we pick the first one with end=3, which might be "ba" at 2 if we iterate by string_index then start.

In our code, we iterate over occurrences in the order we generated:
- first "ba" at 0: (0,0,1)
- then "ba" at 2: (0,2,3) 
- then "ba" at 4: (0,4,5)
- then "aba" at 1: (1,1,3)
- then "aba" at 3: (1,3,5)

So when we check for (string_idx, start, end) in the list, we iterate in this order.

So when at leftmost=2, we check:
- (0,0,1): start=0, end=1 -> 0<=2<=1? false -> skip
- (0,2,3): 2<=2<=3 -> true, end=3 -> best_end=3, best_occur=(0,2,3)
- then (0,4,5): 4<=2? false
- then (1,1,3): 1<=2<=3 -> true, end=3 -> 3 not > 3, so not update
- so we pick (0,2,3)

Then next: leftmost=4
- check (0,0,1): skip
- (0,2,3): end=3, 3<=4? true but 4<=3? false -> not in [start,end] -> skip
- (0,4,5): 4<=4<=5 -> true, end=5 -> best_end=5, so pick (0,4,5)
- then (1,1,3): skip
- (1,3,5): 3<=4<=5 -> true, end=5 -> not greater, so not update

So we get (0,0,1), (0,2,3), (0,4,5)

But if we want to get the expected one, we need to pick (1,1,3) at the second step.

We could modify to choose any with the same end, so we could have chosen (1,1,3) if we iterate and find a later one with same end.

But in our code, we only update when end > best_end, so if two have same end, we pick the first one.

To get the example output, we could change the condition to >=, and pick the one with larger end, and if equal, pick by string index or start? But the problem says "any".

So it's fine.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

Final code:

Note: We have to be careful that the string indices are 0-based in our list, then we add 1.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given some text $t$ and a set of $n$ strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_n$.

In one step, you can choose any occurrence of any string $s_i$ in the text $t$ and color the corresponding characters of the text in red. For example, if $t={bababa}$ and $s_1={ba}$, $s_2={aba}$, you can get $t={{ba}}{baba}$, $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$ or $t={bab}{{aba}}$ in one step.

You want to color all the letters of the text $t$ in red. When you color a letter in red again, it stays red.

In the example above, three steps are enough:

Let's color $t[2 \dots 4]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[1 \dots 2]=s_1={ba}$ in red, we get $t={{baba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[4 \dots 6]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={{bababa}}$.

Each string $s_i$ can be applied any number of times (or not at all). Occurrences for coloring can intersect arbitrarily.

Determine the minimum number of steps needed to color all letters $t$ in red and how to do it. If it is impossible to color all letters of the text $t$ in red, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains an integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 100$) —the number of test cases in the test.

The descriptions of the test cases follow.

The first line of each test case contains the text $t$ ($1 \le |t| \le 100$), consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|t|$ is the length of the text $t$.

The second line of each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10$) — the number of strings in the set.

This is followed by $n$ lines, each containing a string $s_i$ ($1 \le |s_i| \le 10$) consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|s_i|$ — the length of string $s_i$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the answer on a separate line.

If it is impossible to color all the letters of the text in red, print a single line containing the number -1.

Otherwise, on the first line, print the number $m$ — the minimum number of steps it will take to turn all the letters $t$ red.

Then in the next $m$ lines print pairs of indices: $w_j$ and $p_j$ ($1 \le j \le m$), which denote that the string with index $w_j$ was used as a substring to cover the occurrences starting in the text $t$ from position $p_j$. The pairs can be output in any order.

If there are several answers, output any of them.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a text `t` and a set of `n` strings `s_1, s_2, ..., s_n`
2. We can color any occurrence of any string `s_i` in text `t` in red
3. When a character is colored red, it stays red (overlapping is allowed)
4. We want to color ALL characters of text `t` in red with minimum steps
5. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. This is a covering problem - we need to cover the entire text with substrings from our given set
2. We can use any occurrence of any string multiple times
3. We need to find the minimum number of occurrences that cover all positions in `t`
4. This can be modeled as a set cover or interval covering problem
5. Since the text length is at most 100 and string lengths are at most 10, we can use a greedy approach or try to find an optimal solution

## Approach

1. **Check if solution exists**: For the entire text to be covered, every position must be covered by at least one occurrence of some string from our set.
2. **Greedy algorithm**: 
   - Find all possible occurrences of all strings in the text
   - Use a greedy approach: repeatedly select the occurrence that covers the most uncovered positions
   - Continue until all positions are covered or we can't cover any more
3. **Alternative approach**: Since the constraints are small (|t| ≤ 100), we can try a more systematic approach using backtracking or try to find overlapping patterns.

However, given the small constraints, I'll use a greedy approach with refinement:

1. Create a boolean array to track which positions in text are already covered
2. While there are uncovered positions:
   - Find all possible occurrences of all strings that can cover uncovered positions
   - Among these, pick the one that covers the maximum number of currently uncovered positions
   - Mark those positions as covered
   - Record the choice
3. If at the end, not all positions are covered, return -1

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def solve():
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    # Convert text to list of characters for easier position handling (we'll use 1-indexed positions)
    len_t = len(t)
    
    # Find all possible occurrences of all strings
    # Each occurrence is (string_index, start_position)
    # We'll use 1-indexed positions for output
    
    def get_occurrences(text, string_idx):
        string = strings[string_idx]
        occurrences = []
        len_s = len(string)
        len_t = len(text)
        for i in range(len_t - len_s + 1):
            if text[i:i + len_s] == string:
                occurrences.append((i, i + len_s))  # (start, end) in 0-indexed
        return occurrences
    
    # Current coverage: True if position is covered
    covered = [False] * len_t
    steps = []
    
    # Greedy: while there are uncovered positions, find the occurrence that covers the most uncovered positions
    while not all(covered):
        best_gain = -1
        best_occurrence = None
        best_string_idx = -1
        best_start_pos = -1
        
        # Try each string
        for string_idx in range(n):
            string = strings[string_idx]
            len_s = len(string)
            # Get all occurrences of this string
            occurrences = get_occurrences(t, string_idx)
            
            for start, end in occurrences:
                # Check how many positions in this occurrence are currently uncovered
                uncovered_in_this = 0
                for pos in range(start, end):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        uncovered_in_this += 1
                
                if uncovered_in_this > best_gain:
                    best_gain = uncovered_in_this
                    best_occurrence = (start, end)
                    best_string_idx = string_idx
                    best_start_pos = start
        
        # If no occurrence covers any uncovered position, it's impossible
        if best_gain == 0:
            return -1
            
        # Apply this occurrence
        start, end = best_occurrence
        for pos in range(start, end):
            covered[pos] = True
        
        # Record the step
        steps.append((best_string_idx + 1, start + 1))  # Convert to 1-indexed
    
    return steps

def main():
    q = int(input().strip())
    for _ in range(q):
        result = solve()
        if result == -1:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(len(result))
            for step in result:
                print(step[0], step[1])

main()
```

```python
def solve():
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    len_t = len(t)
    covered = [False] * len_t
    steps = []
    
    while not all(covered):
        best_gain = -1
        best_string_idx = -1
        best_start = -1
        
        # Try each string
        for string_idx in range(n):
            string = strings[string_idx]
            len_s = len(string)
            # Find all occurrences of this string
            occurrences = []
            for start in range(len_t - len_s + 1):
                if t[start:start + len_s] == string:
                    occurrences.append(start)
            
            # For each occurrence, calculate how many uncovered positions it covers
            for start in occurrences:
                uncovered_count = 0
                end = start + len_s
                for pos in range(start, end):
                    if not covered[pos]:
                        uncovered_count += 1
                
                if uncovered_count > best_gain:
                    best_gain = uncovered_count
                    best_string_idx = string_idx
                    best_start = start
        
        # If no occurrence covers any uncovered position, impossible
        if best_gain == 0:
            return -1
            
        # Apply this occurrence
        start = best_start
        end = start + len_s
        for pos in range(start, end):
            covered[pos] = True
            
        # Record the step (convert to 1-indexed)
        steps.append((best_string_idx + 1, start + 1))
    
    return steps
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given some text $t$ and a set of $n$ strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_n$.

In one step, you can choose any occurrence of any string $s_i$ in the text $t$ and color the corresponding characters of the text in red. For example, if $t={bababa}$ and $s_1={ba}$, $s_2={aba}$, you can get $t={{ba}}{baba}$, $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$ or $t={bab}{{aba}}$ in one step.

You want to color all the letters of the text $t$ in red. When you color a letter in red again, it stays red.

In the example above, three steps are enough:

Let's color $t[2 \dots 4]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[1 \dots 2]=s_1={ba}$ in red, we get $t={{baba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[4 \dots 6]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={{bababa}}$.

Each string $s_i$ can be applied any number of times (or not at all). Occurrences for coloring can intersect arbitrarily.

Determine the minimum number of steps needed to color all letters $t$ in red and how to do it. If it is impossible to color all letters of the text $t$ in red, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains an integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 100$) —the number of test cases in the test.

The descriptions of the test cases follow.

The first line of each test case contains the text $t$ ($1 \le |t| \le 100$), consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|t|$ is the length of the text $t$.

The second line of each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10$) — the number of strings in the set.

This is followed by $n$ lines, each containing a string $s_i$ ($1 \le |s_i| \le 10$) consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|s_i|$ — the length of string $s_i$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the answer on a separate line.

If it is impossible to color all the letters of the text in red, print a single line containing the number -1.

Otherwise, on the first line, print the number $m$ — the minimum number of steps it will take to turn all the letters $t$ red.

Then in the next $m$ lines print pairs of indices: $w_j$ and $p_j$ ($1 \le j \le m$), which denote that the string with index $w_j$ was used as a substring to cover the occurrences starting in the text $t$ from position $p_j$. The pairs can be output in any order.

If there are several answers, output any of them.

  - Choose (5,6)
  - Total: 3 intervals → matches expected answer

But we have to make sure we actually cover the required positions.

Actually, the standard algorithm for "minimum intervals to cover a line" is:
- Sort intervals by end point
- Greedy: for each uncovered position, pick the interval that covers it and has the maximum end point

But the positions we care about are continuous from 1 to len(t).

So algorithm:
1. Generate all intervals (start, end, string_idx) for each string
2. Sort intervals by end point (end is end+1 in 1-indexed)
3. Initialize covered = [False] * (len(t)+1)  # 1-indexed, position i is covered at index i
4. steps = []
5. current_pos = 1
6. While current_pos <= len(t):
   - Find all intervals that cover current_pos and have end >= current_pos
   - Among these, pick the one with maximum end
   - If no such interval exists, return -1
   - Add it to steps
   - Mark all positions from start to end as covered
   - current_pos = end + 1
7. Return steps

Wait, in the greedy covering, after we pick an interval, we should set current_pos to end + 1, because all positions up to end are covered.

Let me refine:
- current_pos starts at 1
- While current_pos <= len(t):
  - Look for any interval that covers current_pos (meaning start <= current_pos <= end)
  - Among all such intervals, pick the one with maximum end point
  - If none found, impossible
  - Add this interval to solution
  - Mark all positions from start to end as covered
  - Set current_pos = end + 1 (so we start checking from next uncovered position)

But is this optimal? Yes, because by choosing the interval that extends furthest, we maximize coverage for the next step.

Let me test with the first example:
- t = "bababa" (length 6)
- Intervals: (1,2,1), (3,4,1), (5,6,1), (2,4,2), (4,6,2)
- Sort by end: (1,2) end=2, (3,4) end=4, (2,4) end=4, (5,6) end=6, (4,6) end=6
- current_pos = 1
  - Intervals covering 1: (1,2) → end=2
  - Pick (1,2) with end=2
  - Mark positions 1,2 as covered
  - current_pos = 3
- current_pos = 3
  - Intervals covering 3: (3,4) → covers 3,4; (2,4) → covers 2,3,4 (2 is already covered) → both cover 3
  - Max end: both end at 4 → pick one, say (3,4) end=4
  - Mark positions 3,4 as covered
  - current_pos = 5
- current_pos = 5
  - Intervals covering 5: (5,6) → covers 5,6; (4,6) → covers 4,5,6 (4 is already covered) → (5,6) ends at 6, (4,6) ends at 6 → pick one, say (5,6)
  - Mark 5,6 as covered
  - current_pos = 7 > 6 → done
- Steps: (1,2), (3,4), (5,6) → but the expected answer uses different strings

But the problem doesn't require a specific solution, just minimum steps and any valid solution.

In the example, they show:
- 2 2 → string 2 at position 2 (1-indexed)
- 1 1 → string 1 at position 1 (1-indexed)
- 2 4 → string 2 at position 4 (1-indexed)

Let's check:
- (2,2): string 2="aba", position 2 (1-indexed) → in "bababa", position 2-4 (1-indexed): t[1:4]="aba" → "aba" ✓, covers positions 2,3,4
- (1,1): string 1="ba", position 1 → covers positions 1,2
- (2,4): string 2="aba", position 4 → covers positions 4,5,6 → t[3:6]="aba" ✓

So positions: 
- 1: covered by (1,1) and (2,2)
- 2: covered by (1,1) and (2,2)
- 3: covered by (2,2)
- 4: covered by (2,2) and (2,4)
- 5: covered by (2,4)
- 6: covered by (2,4)

This works, but our greedy solution gives (1,2), (3,4), (5,6) which uses only "ba" strings.

Is that valid? (1,2) is "ba" at pos 1 → covers 1,2
(3,4) is "ba" at pos 3 → covers 3,4  
(5,6) is "ba" at pos 5 → covers 5,6

Yes, this covers everything. And it's 3 steps, which is minimum since we have 6 positions and each step covers at most 2 positions, so minimum 3 steps.

So our algorithm works.

Let me implement the algorithm step by step.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    length_t = len(t)
    # Generate all intervals: (start_pos_1_indexed, end_pos_1_indexed, string_idx)
    intervals = []
    
    for string_idx, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        # Check each starting position in t
        for start in range(len_t - len_s + 1):
            # start is 0-indexed
            start_1_indexed = start + 1
            end_1_indexed = start + len_s  # 1-indexed end position
            if t[start:start + len_s] == s:
                intervals.append((start_1_indexed, end_1_indexed, string_idx))
    
    # If no intervals, impossible
    if not intervals:
        return -1
    
    # Sort intervals by end_1_indexed (descending or ascending? We want to pick those that extend furthest)
    # Actually, for greedy covering, we consider each uncovered position and pick the interval that covers it and has max end
    # So we sort by end descending? No, we'll do the selection directly
    
    # We'll use greedy: current_pos from 1 to length_t
    covered = [False] * (length_t + 1)  # 1-indexed, covered[pos] = True if position pos is covered
    steps = []
    
    current_pos = 1
    while current_pos <= length_t:
        best_end = -1
        best_interval = None
        
        # Find all intervals that cover current_pos
        for start, end, string_idx in intervals:
            if start <= current_pos <= end:
                if end > best_end:
                    best_end = end
                    best_interval = (start, end, string_idx)
        
        if best_interval is None:
            return -1
            
        start, end, string_idx = best_interval
        steps.append((string_idx + 1, start))  # string_idx in 0-indexed, so +1 for output
        
        # Mark all positions from start to end as covered
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
            
        # Move current_pos to end + 1
        current_pos = end + 1
    
    return steps

# Main
q = int(input().strip())
for _ in range(q):
    result = solve()
    if result == -1:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(len(result))
        for step in result:
            print(step[0], step[1])
```

Wait, I think there's a bug. In the greedy step, when we find an interval that covers current_pos, we pick the one with maximum end. But if multiple have the same end, any is fine.

But the algorithm above will work.

- "a" at pos 1: start=2, end=2

- find intervals covering 1: (1,1) covers 1 → end=1

- steps: [(1,1)]
- mark position 1 covered
- current_pos = 2
- current_pos=2 <= 2:
- find intervals covering 2: (2,2) covers 2 → end=2 > current best_end (but we don't have any in this step)
- actually, we check: is there interval covering 2? (2,2) covers 2 → end=2
- so best_end=2, pick (2,2)
- steps: [(1,1), (1,2)]
- then current_pos = 3 > 2 → done

But actually, we only need one step? No, each step covers one character. With "a", we need to cover two positions.

We have two occurrences of "a" in "aa":
- at pos 1: covers position 1

So we need two steps. Is there a way to cover both in one step? No, because each occurrence covers one position.

So minimum is 2.

But wait, can we cover with one occurrence? Only if one string covers multiple positions, but "a" covers one position.

Final code:

```python
def solve():
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    length_t = len(t)
    # Generate all intervals: (start_pos_1_indexed, end_pos_1_indexed, string_idx)
    intervals = []
    
    for string_idx, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        # Check each starting position in t
        for start in range(length_t - len_s + 1):
            # start is 0-indexed
            start_1_indexed = start + 1
            end_1_indexed = start + len_s  # 1-indexed end position
            if t[start:start + len_s] == s:
                intervals.append((start_1_indexed, end_1_indexed, string_idx))
    
    # If no intervals, impossible
    if not intervals:
        return -1
    
    # Sort intervals by end_1_indexed (descending) won't help, we'll find best for each step
    # We'll use greedy: current_pos from 1 to length_t
    covered = [False] * (length_t + 1)  # 1-indexed, covered[pos] = True if position pos is covered
    steps = []
    
    current_pos = 1
    while current_pos <= length_t:
        best_end = -1
        best_interval = None
        
        # Find all intervals that cover current_pos
        for start, end, string_idx in intervals:
            if start <= current_pos <= end:
                if end > best_end:
                    best_end = end
                    best_interval = (start, end, string_idx)
        
        if best_interval is None:
            return -1
            
        start, end, string_idx = best_interval
        steps.append((string_idx + 1, start))  # string_idx in 0-indexed, so +1 for output
        
        # Mark all positions from start to end as covered
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
            
        # Move current_pos to end + 1
        current_pos = end + 1
    
    return steps

# Main
q = int(input().strip())
for _ in range(q):
    result = solve()
    if result == -1:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(len(result))
        for step in result:
            print(step[0], step[1])
```

I believe this is correct based on the problem and examples. The examples might have typos, or the expected outputs might be non-minimal. Our solution finds the minimum number of steps.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def solve():
    t = input().strip()
    n = int(input().strip())
    strings = []
    for _ in range(n):
        strings.append(input().strip())
    
    length_t = len(t)
    # Generate all intervals: (start_pos_1_indexed, end_pos_1_indexed, string_idx)
    intervals = []
    
    for string_idx, s in enumerate(strings):
        len_s = len(s)
        # Check each starting position in t
        for start in range(length_t - len_s + 1):
            # start is 0-indexed
            start_1_indexed = start + 1
            end_1_indexed = start + len_s  # 1-indexed end position
            if t[start:start + len_s] == s:
                intervals.append((start_1_indexed, end_1_indexed, string_idx))
    
    # If no intervals, impossible
    if not intervals:
        return -1
    
    # Sort intervals by end_1_indexed (descending) won't help, we'll find best for each step
    # We'll use greedy: current_pos from 1 to length_t
    covered = [False] * (length_t + 1)  # 1-indexed, covered[pos] = True if position pos is covered
    steps = []
    
    current_pos = 1
    while current_pos <= length_t:
        best_end = -1
        best_interval = None
        
        # Find all intervals that cover current_pos
        for start, end, string_idx in intervals:
            if start <= current_pos <= end:
                if end > best_end:
                    best_end = end
                    best_interval = (start, end, string_idx)
        
        if best_interval is None:
            return -1
            
        start, end, string_idx = best_interval
        steps.append((string_idx + 1, start))  # string_idx in 0-indexed, so +1 for output
        
        # Mark all positions from start to end as covered
        for pos in range(start, end + 1):
            covered[pos] = True
            
        # Move current_pos to end + 1
        current_pos = end + 1
    
    return steps

# Main
q = int(input().strip())
for _ in range(q):
    result = solve()
    if result == -1:
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(len(result))
        for step in result:
            print(step[0], step[1])

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given some text $t$ and a set of $n$ strings $s_1, s_2, \dots, s_n$.

In one step, you can choose any occurrence of any string $s_i$ in the text $t$ and color the corresponding characters of the text in red. For example, if $t={bababa}$ and $s_1={ba}$, $s_2={aba}$, you can get $t={{ba}}{baba}$, $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$ or $t={bab}{{aba}}$ in one step.

You want to color all the letters of the text $t$ in red. When you color a letter in red again, it stays red.

In the example above, three steps are enough:

Let's color $t[2 \dots 4]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={b}{{aba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[1 \dots 2]=s_1={ba}$ in red, we get $t={{baba}}{ba}$;

Let's color $t[4 \dots 6]=s_2={aba}$ in red, we get $t={{bababa}}$.

Each string $s_i$ can be applied any number of times (or not at all). Occurrences for coloring can intersect arbitrarily.

Determine the minimum number of steps needed to color all letters $t$ in red and how to do it. If it is impossible to color all letters of the text $t$ in red, output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains an integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 100$) —the number of test cases in the test.

The descriptions of the test cases follow.

The first line of each test case contains the text $t$ ($1 \le |t| \le 100$), consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|t|$ is the length of the text $t$.

The second line of each test case contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \le n \le 10$) — the number of strings in the set.

This is followed by $n$ lines, each containing a string $s_i$ ($1 \le |s_i| \le 10$) consisting only of lowercase Latin letters, where $|s_i|$ — the length of string $s_i$.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print the answer on a separate line.

If it is impossible to color all the letters of the text in red, print a single line containing the number -1.

Otherwise, on the first line, print the number $m$ — the minimum number of steps it will take to turn all the letters $t$ red.

Then in the next $m$ lines print pairs of indices: $w_j$ and $p_j$ ($1 \le j \le m$), which denote that the string with index $w_j$ was used as a substring to cover the occurrences starting in the text $t$ from position $p_j$. The pairs can be output in any order.

If there are several answers, output any of them.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a text `t` and a set of `n` strings `s_1, s_2, ..., s_n`
2. In one step, we can choose any occurrence of any string `s_i` in text `t` and color its characters red
3. Colorings can overlap (repeated coloring doesn't change the result)
4. We want to color ALL characters of text `t` in red with minimum steps
5. If impossible, output -1

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a covering problem - we need to cover the entire text with substrings from our given set
2. Each step can cover an occurrence of any string from our set
3. We want to minimize the number of such occurrences
4. This is similar to a set cover problem, but with the constraint that we can only use substrings from our given set

## Approach

1. For each position in the text, we need to find if any of our strings can cover it
2. We can use BFS or greedy approach to find minimum coverage
3. However, since the text length is at most 100 and string length at most 10, we can try a greedy or backtracking approach
4. Actually, this is more like a "minimum set cover" with specific substrings

Wait, let me think more systematically:

Since the constraints are small (|t| ≤ 100, n ≤ 10, |s_i| ≤ 10), we can use a greedy approach:

1. Start with the entire text as uncovered
2. At each step, try to find the occurrence of any string that covers the maximum number of currently uncovered positions
3. Mark those positions as covered and repeat
4. If we can't cover everything, return -1

But there's a catch - the greedy might not be optimal, but given the small constraints, it might work.

Alternatively, we can use BFS to try all possible combinations, but that would be exponential.

Another idea: since the text is small, we can use DFS/backtracking with pruning to find minimum steps.

But simpler: use a greedy algorithm that at each step picks the substring occurrence that covers the most uncovered positions.

However, let me verify if greedy works with the examples.

- If we pick "ba" at position 2: covers 2,3
- After "aba" at pos 1: covered 1,2,3
- Then "ba" at pos 0: covers 0,1 (only 0 is new) 
- Then "aba" at pos 3: covers 3,4,5 (only 4,5 new)
- This gives 3 steps.

- ba at position 0: covers 0,1 → positions: b,a  
- aba at position 3: covers 3,4,5 → positions: a,b,a

After first step (aba at pos 1): covered 1,2,3

Algorithm:
1. Convert text to list of boolean flags (uncovered)
2. While there are uncovered positions:
   a. Find all possible occurrences of all strings
   b. For each occurrence, calculate how many new uncovered positions it would cover
   c. Choose the occurrence that covers the most new uncovered positions
   d. Mark those positions as covered
3. If we can't cover any position in a step, return -1
4. Return the sequence of occurrences

So the algorithm is:
1. For each test case:
   - Read text t, n, and n strings
   - Initialize uncovered array (boolean list of True)
   - steps = []
   - While any position is uncovered:
      - Find the best occurrence (by maximizing new uncovered positions covered)
      - If no occurrence covers any uncovered position, return -1
      - Add the chosen occurrence to steps
      - Mark the covered positions as not uncovered
   - Return steps

But how to find "best" - we need to try all possible occurrences of all strings.

Edge cases:
- If the text is empty, but |t| ≥ 1 by constraints
- If we have a string that occurs, find all starting positions where it matches the substring

```python
def solve():
    q = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(q):
        t = input().strip()
        n = int(input())
        strings = []
        for _ in range(n):
            strings.append(input().strip())
        
        # Try to cover the entire text
        uncovered = [True] * len(t)
        steps = []
        
        # While there are uncovered positions
        changed = True
        while changed:
            changed = False
            best_gain = -1
            best_occurrence = None  # (string_idx, start_pos_0_indexed)
            
            # Try all strings
            for string_idx, s in enumerate(strings):
                len_s = len(s)
                # Try all starting positions where this string could fit
                for start in range(len(t) - len_s + 1):
                    # Check if s matches t[start:start+len_s]
                    match = True
                    for i in range(len_s):
                        if t[start + i] != s[i]:
                            match = False
                            break
                    
                    if match:
                        # Count how many uncovered positions this occurrence covers
                        gain = 0
                        for i in range(len_s):
                            pos = start + i
                            if uncovered[pos]:
                                gain += 1
                        if gain > best_gain:
                            best_gain = gain
                            best_occurrence = (string_idx, start)
            
            # If no occurrence covers any uncovered position, impossible
            if best_gain == 0:
                steps = []
                break
                
            # Otherwise, we found a good occurrence
            string_idx, start = best_occurrence
            # Mark the positions covered by this occurrence
            for i in range(len(strings[string_idx])):
                pos = start + i
                if uncovered[pos]:
                    uncovered[pos] = False
                    changed = True
            
            steps.append((string_idx + 1, start + 1))  # convert to 1-indexed
            
        # Check if all positions are covered
        all_covered = all(not uncovered[i] for i in range(len(t)))
        
        if not all_covered:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(len(steps))
            for step in steps:
                print(step[0], step[1])

solve()
```
