I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment:
   - Start with 1 bacterium
   - Each second: each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more are added
   - After n seconds, we have exactly z bacteria

2. In the second experiment:
   - Start with t bacteria
   - Same growth rule: each second, x bacteria become kx + b bacteria
   - We need to find minimum seconds to get at least z bacteria

3. We need to find the minimum number of seconds for the second experiment to reach at least z bacteria.

## Step 1: Find z from the first experiment

First, I need to compute what z is based on the first experiment parameters (k, b, n).

Starting with 1 bacterium:
- After 0 seconds: 1
- After 1 second: k×1 + b = k + b
- After 2 seconds: k×(k + b) + b = k² + kb + b
- After 3 seconds: k×(k² + kb + b) + b = k³ + k²b + kb + b

The pattern is: after n seconds, we have kⁿ + b×(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k + 1)

This is a geometric series: kⁿ + b×(kⁿ - 1)/(k - 1) when k > 1

If k = 1: then it's 1 + b×n

So we need to compute z = f(k, b, n) where:
- If k = 1: z = 1 + b×n
- If k > 1: z = kⁿ + b×(kⁿ - 1)/(k - 1)

## Step 2: Find minimum seconds for second experiment

Starting with t bacteria, after s seconds we have g(t, k, b, s) bacteria.

We need to find the minimum s such that g(t, k, b, s) ≥ z.

The recurrence is:
- After 0 seconds: t
- After 1 second: k×t + b
- After 2 seconds: k×(k×t + b) + b = k²×t + k×b + b
- After s seconds: k^s × t + b×(k^(s-1) + k^(s-2) + ... + k + 1)

This simplifies to:
- If k = 1: t + b×s
- If k > 1: t×k^s + b×(k^s - 1)/(k - 1)

We need to find minimum s ≥ 0 such that the above expression ≥ z.

Note: s can be 0 (if t ≥ z)

## Step 3: Implementation strategy

1. Calculate z from first experiment
2. For s = 0, 1, 2, ... check if the bacteria count ≥ z
3. Return the first s where this is true

But we need to be careful about overflow and efficiency. Since n and t can be up to 10^6, we can't compute k^n directly if k is large.

However, we can compute the geometric series formulas carefully.

For k > 1:
- z = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1) = (k^n * (k - 1) + b * (k^n - 1)) / (k - 1)
- Actually: k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1) = k^n + b * (k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + 1)

Wait, let me recalculate:
After 1 second: k×1 + b
After 2 seconds: k×(k×1 + b) + b = k² + kb + b
After 3 seconds: k×(k² + kb + b) + b = k³ + k²b + kb + b

So after n seconds: k^n + b×(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^1 + k^0)
= k^n + b×(k^n - 1)/(k - 1)

Yes, that's correct.

For second experiment with initial t:
After s seconds: t×k^s + b×(k^s - 1)/(k - 1) when k > 1
After s seconds: t×k^s + b×(k^(s-1) + k^(s-2) + ... + k^0) = t×k^s + b×(k^s - 1)/(k - 1)

For k = 1: 
- First experiment: 1 + b×n
- Second experiment: t + b×s

## Step 4: Edge cases

If t ≥ z, then s = 0.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
First experiment: z = 3³ + 1×(3³ - 1)/(3-1) = 27 + (27-1)/2 = 27 + 26/2 = 27 + 13 = 40
Second experiment: start with 5, need to reach at least 40
s=0: 5 < 40
s=1: 3×5 + 1 = 16 < 40  
s=2: 3×16 + 1 = 49 ≥ 40 ✓
Answer: 2 ✓

Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
First experiment: z = 1 + 4×4 = 17
Second experiment: start with 7, need ≥ 17
s=0: 7 < 17
s=1: 7 + 4×1 = 11 < 17
s=2: 7 + 4×2 = 15 < 17
s=3: 7 + 4×3 = 19 ≥ 17 ✓
Answer: 3 ✓

Example 3: k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
First experiment: z = 2⁴ + 2×(2⁴ - 1)/(2-1) = 16 + 2×(16-1)/1 = 16 + 2×15 = 16 + 30 = 46
Second experiment: start with 100 ≥ 46, so s=0 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Calculate z from first experiment
    if k == 1:
        z = 1 + b * n
    else:
        # k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)
        k_n = pow(k, n)
        z = k_n + b * (k_n - 1) // (k - 1)
    
    # Find minimum seconds for second experiment
    if t >= z:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # For k == 1: after s seconds, bacteria = t + b * s
    # For k > 1: after s seconds, bacteria = t * k^s + b * (k^s - 1) // (k - 1)
    
    if k == 1:
        # t + b * s >= z
        # s >= (z - t) / b
        s = (z - t + b - 1) // b  # ceiling division
        print(s)
    else:
        s = 0
        current = t
        while current < z:
            s += 1
            # Update current: k * current + b
            # But we need to be careful about growth
            # After s seconds: t * k^s + b * (k^s - 1) / (k - 1)
            # We can simulate directly since s won't be too large
            # Worst case: if k=1 we already handled, if k>1 and t is small, s might be large
            # But looking at the constraints, since z grows exponentially, s won't be huge
            
            # We can simulate step by step
            current = k * current + b
        
        print(s)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be careful. In the simulation, when k > 1, each second we multiply by k and add b.

But is this equivalent to the formula? Let me verify:
- s=0: t (correct)
- s=1: k*t + b (correct)
- s=2: k*(k*t + b) + b = k²*t + k*b + b (correct)

And the formula is: t*k^s + b*(k^s - 1)/(k-1)

For s=1: t*k + b*(k-1)/(k-1) = t*k + b ✓
For s=2: t*k² + b*(k²-1)/(k-1) = t*k² + b*(k+1) = t*k² + b*k + b ✓

Yes, it's correct.

However, what if s becomes very large? The number might be huge, but since we're only interested in whether it's ≥ z, and z itself is computed from n (≤ 10^6), with k≥2, k^n grows very fast, so s will be small.

The worst case is when k is large and n is large, z is very large, but t is small, so we need many seconds? Actually, no - since k≥2, even for k=2, it grows exponentially.

But k could be 1, which we handle separately.

For k>1, the growth is exponential, so s won't exceed about 60 or so in practice. So simulation is safe.

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
if k == 1:
    z = 1 + b * n
else:
    k_n = pow(k, n)
    z = k_n + b * (k_n - 1) // (k - 1)

# Find minimum seconds for second experiment
if t >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    if k == 1:
        # s >= (z - t) / b
        s = (z - t + b - 1) // b  # ceiling division
        print(s)
    else:
        s = 0
        current = t
        while current < z:
            s += 1
            current = k * current + b
        print(s)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Qwerty the Ranger took up a government job and arrived on planet Mars. He should stay in the secret lab and conduct some experiments on bacteria that have funny and abnormal properties. The job isn't difficult, but the salary is high.

At the beginning of the first experiment there is a single bacterium in the test tube. Every second each bacterium in the test tube divides itself into k bacteria. After that some abnormal effects create b more bacteria in the test tube. Thus, if at the beginning of some second the test tube had x bacteria, then at the end of the second it will have kx + b bacteria.

The experiment showed that after n seconds there were exactly z bacteria and the experiment ended at this point.

For the second experiment Qwerty is going to sterilize the test tube and put there t bacteria. He hasn't started the experiment yet but he already wonders, how many seconds he will need to grow at least z bacteria. The ranger thinks that the bacteria will divide by the same rule as in the first experiment. 

Help Qwerty and find the minimum number of seconds needed to get a tube with at least z bacteria in the second experiment.

Input

The first line contains four space-separated integers k, b, n and t (1 ≤ k, b, n, t ≤ 106) — the parameters of bacterial growth, the time Qwerty needed to grow z bacteria in the first experiment and the initial number of bacteria in the second experiment, correspondingly.

Output

Print a single number — the minimum number of seconds Qwerty needs to grow at least z bacteria in the tube.

Examples

Input

3 1 3 5


Output

2

Input

1 4 4 7


Output

3

Input

2 2 4 100


Output

0

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment:
   - Start with 1 bacterium
   - Each second: each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more are added
   - After n seconds, we have exactly z bacteria

2. In the second experiment:
   - Start with t bacteria
   - Same growth rule: each second, x becomes k*x + b
   - We need to find the minimum seconds to get at least z bacteria

3. Goal: Find minimum seconds needed in the second experiment to reach at least z bacteria.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find z from the first experiment
We need to simulate the first experiment to find the final count z after n seconds.

Starting with 1 bacterium:
- After 0 seconds: 1
- After 1 second: k*1 + b = k + b
- After 2 seconds: k*(k + b) + b = k² + kb + b
- After n seconds: we need to compute this recursively

We can compute this iteratively:
- initial = 1
- for i in range(n): initial = k * initial + b

This gives us z.

### Step 2: Simulate the second experiment
We start with t bacteria and apply the same growth rule each second until we reach at least z bacteria.

We need to find the minimum number of seconds.

### Step 3: Handle edge cases
- If t >= z, then we need 0 seconds (already have enough)
- If t < z, we need to simulate growth until we reach at least z

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
- First experiment: start with 1
  - After 1s: 3*1 + 1 = 4
  - After 2s: 3*4 + 1 = 13
  - After 3s: 3*13 + 1 = 40
  - So z = 40
- Second experiment: start with t=5
  - After 0s: 5 < 40, need more
  - After 1s: 3*5 + 1 = 16 < 40
  - After 2s: 3*16 + 1 = 49 >= 40
  - So answer is 2 ✓

**Example 2:** k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
- First experiment: start with 1
  - After 1s: 1*1 + 4 = 5
  - After 2s: 1*5 + 4 = 9
  - After 3s: 1*9 + 4 = 13
  - After 4s: 1*13 + 4 = 17
  - So z = 17
- Second experiment: start with t=7
  - After 0s: 7 < 17, need more
  - After 1s: 1*7 + 4 = 11 < 17
  - After 2s: 1*11 + 4 = 15 < 17
  - After 3s: 1*15 + 4 = 19 >= 17
  - So answer is 3 ✓

**Example 3:** k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
- First experiment: start with 1
  - After 1s: 2*1 + 2 = 4
  - After 2s: 2*4 + 2 = 10
  - After 3s: 2*10 + 2 = 22
  - After 4s: 2*22 + 2 = 46
  - So z = 46
- Second experiment: start with t=100
  - 100 >= 46, so answer is 0 ✓

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment (starting with 1 bacterium for n seconds)
z = 1
for _ in range(n):
    z = k * z + b

# Find minimum seconds needed in second experiment starting with t bacteria
current = t
seconds = 0

# If we already have at least z bacteria, no time needed
if current >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    # Otherwise simulate each second until we reach at least z
    while current < z:
        current = k * current + b
        seconds += 1
    print(seconds)
```

The algorithm:
1. Calculate z by simulating the first experiment starting with 1 bacterium for n seconds
2. Simulate the second experiment starting with t bacteria, counting seconds until we reach at least z
3. Return the number of seconds needed

Time complexity: O(n + s) where s is the number of seconds in the second experiment, which is reasonable given the constraints.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment, we start with 1 bacterium and after n seconds, we have exactly z bacteria.
2. The growth rule is: each second, each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more bacteria are added.
3. In the second experiment, we start with t bacteria and want to find the minimum time to get at least z bacteria.
4. The growth rule is the same.

## Step 1: Find z from the first experiment

First, I need to calculate what z is (the final number of bacteria after n seconds in the first experiment).

The recurrence is:
- At time 0: 1 bacterium
- At time 1: k×1 + b = k + b
- At time 2: k×(k + b) + b = k² + kb + b
- At time i: k×(previous) + b

This forms a recurrence relation. I can solve this mathematically:
f(0) = 1
f(i) = k×f(i-1) + b

The closed form for this recurrence is:
f(n) = k^n + b×(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^0) = k^n + b×(k^n - 1)/(k - 1) when k > 1

When k = 1, the recurrence becomes f(i) = 1 + i×b, so f(n) = 1 + n×b

So:
- If k = 1: z = 1 + n×b
- If k > 1: z = k^n + b×(k^n - 1)/(k - 1)

## Step 2: Find minimum time for second experiment

Once I have z, I need to find the minimum time s such that starting with t bacteria, after s seconds, we have at least z bacteria.

The recurrence for the second experiment:
- Start with t bacteria
- After 0 seconds: t
- After 1 second: k×t + b
- After 2 seconds: k×(k×t + b) + b = k²×t + k×b + b
- After s seconds: k^s × t + b × (k^(s-1) + k^(s-2) + ... + k^0) = k^s × t + b × (k^s - 1) / (k - 1) when k > 1

When k = 1, it becomes: t + s×b

So I need to find minimum s such that:
- If k = 1: t + s×b ≥ z
- If k > 1: k^s × t + b × (k^s - 1) / (k - 1) ≥ z

## Step 3: Implementation approach

1. Calculate z from the first experiment using the recurrence or formula
2. For the second experiment, iterate s from 0 upwards until we reach at least z bacteria
3. Since t ≥ 1 and k ≥ 1, the values grow exponentially (except when k=1), so we won't have an infinite loop

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
- First experiment: start with 1
  - After 0: 1
  - After 1: 3×1 + 1 = 4
  - After 2: 3×4 + 1 = 13
  - After 3: 3×13 + 1 = 40
  - So z = 40
- Second experiment: start with 5
  - After 0: 5 < 40 → no
  - After 1: 3×5 + 1 = 16 < 40 → no
  - After 2: 3×16 + 1 = 49 ≥ 40 → yes
  - Answer: 2 ✓

Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
- First experiment: start with 1
  - Since k=1: z = 1 + 4×4 = 17
- Second experiment: start with 7
  - k=1, so after s seconds: 7 + s×4
  - After 0: 7 < 17
  - After 1: 11 < 17
  - After 2: 15 < 17
  - After 3: 19 ≥ 17 → answer 3 ✓

Example 3: k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
- First experiment: start with 1
  - After 0: 1
  - After 1: 2×1 + 2 = 4
  - After 2: 2×4 + 2 = 10
  - After 3: 2×10 + 2 = 22
  - After 4: 2×22 + 2 = 46
  - So z = 46
- Second experiment: start with 100
  - After 0: 100 ≥ 46 → so answer is 0 ✓

Wait, this seems to contradict. The example says output is 0. If we already have 100 bacteria and z=46, then yes, 100 ≥ 46, so we need 0 seconds.

But let me recalculate the first experiment with k=2, b=2, n=4:
- Start: 1
- After 1: 2×1 + 2 = 4
- After 2: 2×4 + 2 = 10
- After 3: 2×10 + 2 = 22
- After 4: 2×22 + 2 = 46 ✓

So yes, answer is 0.

## Implementation

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
def calculate_z(k, b, n):
    if k == 1:
        return 1 + n * b
    else:
        # f(n) = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)
        k_power_n = pow(k, n)
        return k_power_n + b * (k_power_n - 1) // (k - 1)

z = calculate_z(k, b, n)

# Find minimum seconds for second experiment
current = t
seconds = 0

while current < z:
    current = k * current + b
    seconds += 1

print(seconds)
```

Wait, let me check if this works for the edge case where t is already >= z. Yes, the while loop will skip it and return 0.

Let me verify with example 2 again:
- k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
- z = 1 + 4*4 = 17
- current = 7, seconds = 0
- 7 < 17 → current = 1*7 + 4 = 11, seconds = 1
- 11 < 17 → current = 1*11 + 4 = 15, seconds = 2
- 15 < 17 → current = 1*15 + 4 = 19, seconds = 3
- 19 >= 17 → exit, return 3 ✓

Perfect.

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
def calculate_z(k, b, n):
    if k == 1:
        return 1 + n * b
    else:
        k_power_n = pow(k, n)
        return k_power_n + b * (k_power_n - 1) // (k - 1)

z = calculate_z(k, b, n)

# Find minimum seconds for second experiment
current = t
seconds = 0

while current < z:
    current = k * current + b
    seconds += 1

print(seconds)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment, we start with 1 bacterium and after n seconds, we have exactly z bacteria.
2. The growth rule: each second, each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more are added.
3. In the second experiment, we start with t bacteria and want to find the minimum seconds to get at least z bacteria.
4. We need to find this minimum time.

## Step 1: Find z from the first experiment

First, I need to calculate z (the final number of bacteria after n seconds in the first experiment).

The recurrence relation is:
- Start: 1 bacterium
- After each second: x → k*x + b
- After n seconds: z = f(n) where f(0) = 1, f(i) = k*f(i-1) + b

This is a linear recurrence. I can compute it iteratively or find a closed form.

The closed form for f(n) = k^n + b*(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^0) = k^n + b*(k^n - 1)/(k - 1) for k > 1
For k = 1: f(n) = 1 + b*n

Let me verify with the examples:
- Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3
  - f(0) = 1
  - f(1) = 3*1 + 1 = 4
  - f(2) = 3*4 + 1 = 13
  - f(3) = 3*13 + 1 = 40
  - So z = 40
- After 2 seconds with t=5: 
  - Start: 5
  - After 1 second: 3*5 + 1 = 16
  - After 2 seconds: 3*16 + 1 = 49 ≥ 40
  - So answer is 2 ✓

- Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4
  - For k=1: f(n) = 1 + b*n = 1 + 4*4 = 17
  - Start with t=7, want at least 17
  - After 0 seconds: 7 < 17
  - After 1 second: 1*7 + 4 = 11 < 17
  - After 2 seconds: 1*11 + 4 = 15 < 17
  - After 3 seconds: 1*15 + 4 = 19 ≥ 17
  - So answer is 3 ✓

## Step 2: Algorithm

1. Calculate z (final bacteria count after n seconds in first experiment)
2. For the second experiment with t bacteria, simulate the process second by second until we have at least z bacteria
3. Return the number of seconds

## Step 3: Implementation

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
# Start with 1 bacterium
current = 1
for _ in range(n):
    current = k * current + b

z = current

# Now simulate second experiment starting with t bacteria
current = t
seconds = 0

# If we already have at least z bacteria, return 0
if current >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    # Simulate each second
    while current < z:
        current = k * current + b
        seconds += 1
    print(seconds)
```

Wait, let me check example 3:
- Input: 2 2 4 100
- First experiment: k=2, b=2, n=4
  - f(0) = 1
  - f(1) = 2*1 + 2 = 4
  - f(2) = 2*4 + 2 = 10
  - f(3) = 2*10 + 2 = 22
  - f(4) = 2*22 + 2 = 46
  - So z = 46
- Second experiment: start with t=100
- Since 100 ≥ 46, answer should be 0 ✓

This matches. But the constraint says n, t ≤ 10^6, so the simulation might take many iterations in the worst case.

Let me think about worst case: if k=1, b=1, n is large, and t is very small, then we might need many iterations. However, since the growth is exponential (k>=1), the bacteria grow rapidly, so the number of iterations should be reasonable.

But let's analyze: if k=1, then growth is linear: each second we add b bacteria. So if we start with t and want to reach z, we need (z - t) / b seconds (if z > t). Since z is determined by the first experiment and n, and n ≤ 10^6, z might be very large, and (z-t)/b could be large.

Actually, in the worst case where k=1, b=1, and n=10^6, then z = 1 + 1*10^6 = 10^6 + 1, which is about 10^6.

If t=1, then we need (10^6 + 1 - 1) / 1 = 10^6 seconds. That's 10^6 iterations, which might be acceptable in Python if optimized (10^6 is acceptable in most cases).

But what if k=1, b=10^6, n=10^6? Then z = 1 + 10^6 * 10^6 = 10^12 + 1. If t=1, then we need (10^12) / 10^6 = 10^6 seconds. Still 10^6 iterations.

In the worst case, the number of iterations needed might be up to 10^6, which is acceptable in Python.

So the simulation approach should work.

Let me code it:

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
current = 1
for _ in range(n):
    current = k * current + b

z = current

# Simulate second experiment
current = t
seconds = 0

if current >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    while current < z:
        current = k * current + b
        seconds += 1
    print(seconds)
```

Wait, but what if k = 1 and b is large? The while loop might need many iterations if z is very large. But z grows linearly with n, and n ≤ 10^6, so z ≤ 10^6 * 10^6 = 10^12 in worst case. 

With k=1, the growth per second is only b. So if t is small and z is large, we need about (z - t) / b seconds. In worst case b=1, z=10^12, t=1, we need 10^12 seconds, which is too many iterations.

This is a problem! I need to handle the case where k=1 more efficiently.

For k=1: growth is linear each second: x → x + b
So after s seconds: t + s * b

We want t + s * b ≥ z
So s ≥ (z - t) / b
If z ≤ t: s = 0
If z > t: s = ceil((z - t) / b)

For k > 1: growth is exponential, so we can simulate until we reach z, and since exponential growth, we won't need many iterations even if z is large.

So I need to handle k=1 and k>1 separately.

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
current = 1
for _ in range(n):
    current = k * current + b

z = current

# If we already have at least z bacteria
if t >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    if k == 1:
        # For k=1, growth is linear: each second adds b bacteria
        # After s seconds: t + s * b >= z
        # s >= (z - t) / b
        needed = z - t
        if needed <= 0:
            print(0)
        else:
            # s must be at least ceil(needed / b)
            s = (needed + b - 1) // b
            print(s)
    else:
        # For k > 1, we can simulate since exponential growth
        current = t
        seconds = 0
        while current < z:
            current = k * current + b
            seconds += 1
        print(seconds)
```

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
- z = 40 (as calculated)
- t=5 < 40, k>1
- seconds: 
  - start: 5
  - after 1: 3*5 + 1 = 16
  - after 2: 3*16 + 1 = 49 ≥ 40
  - seconds = 2 ✓

Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
- z = 1 + 4*4 = 17
- t=7 < 17, k=1
- needed = 17 - 7 = 10
- s = (10 + 4 - 1) // 4 = 13 // 4 = 3 ✓

Example 3: k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
- z = 46 (as calculated)
- t=100 ≥ 46, so print 0 ✓

All examples pass.

Edge case: if k=1 and z <= t, we print 0.

The formula for ceiling division: ceil(a/b) = (a + b - 1) // b when a > 0

If a <= 0, ceil(a/b) = 0 or negative, but in our case needed = z - t > 0 since we're in the else branch where t < z.

So the code is:

```python
k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate z from first experiment
current = 1
for _ in range(n):
    current = k * current + b

z = current

# If we already have at least z bacteria
if t >= z:
    print(0)
else:
    if k == 1:
        # For k=1, growth is linear: each second adds b bacteria
        needed = z - t
        s = (needed + b - 1) // b
        print(s)
    else:
        # For k > 1, we can simulate since exponential growth
        current = t
        seconds = 0
        while current < z:
            current = k * current + b
            seconds += 1
        print(seconds)
```

This should handle all cases efficiently.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Qwerty the Ranger took up a government job and arrived on planet Mars. He should stay in the secret lab and conduct some experiments on bacteria that have funny and abnormal properties. The job isn't difficult, but the salary is high.

At the beginning of the first experiment there is a single bacterium in the test tube. Every second each bacterium in the test tube divides itself into k bacteria. After that some abnormal effects create b more bacteria in the test tube. Thus, if at the beginning of some second the test tube had x bacteria, then at the end of the second it will have kx + b bacteria.

The experiment showed that after n seconds there were exactly z bacteria and the experiment ended at this point.

For the second experiment Qwerty is going to sterilize the test tube and put there t bacteria. He hasn't started the experiment yet but he already wonders, how many seconds he will need to grow at least z bacteria. The ranger thinks that the bacteria will divide by the same rule as in the first experiment. 

Help Qwerty and find the minimum number of seconds needed to get a tube with at least z bacteria in the second experiment.

The first line contains four space-separated integers k, b, n and t (1 ≤ k, b, n, t ≤ 106) — the parameters of bacterial growth, the time Qwerty needed to grow z bacteria in the first experiment and the initial number of bacteria in the second experiment, correspondingly.

Output

Print a single number — the minimum number of seconds Qwerty needs to grow at least z bacteria in the tube.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment:
   - Start with 1 bacterium
   - Each second: each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more are added
   - After n seconds, there are exactly z bacteria

2. In the second experiment:
   - Start with t bacteria
   - Same growth rule: each second, each bacterium becomes k bacteria, then b more are added
   - We need to find the minimum number of seconds to get at least z bacteria

3. We need to first determine z (the target number of bacteria from the first experiment), then calculate how many seconds are needed from the second experiment.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate z from the first experiment

In the first experiment, we start with 1 bacterium and apply the growth rule n times.

Let's trace the growth:
- Start: 1
- After 1 second: k×1 + b = k + b
- After 2 seconds: k×(k + b) + b = k² + kb + b
- After 3 seconds: k×(k² + kb + b) + b = k³ + k²b + kb + b

So after n seconds: z = kⁿ + b×(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k + 1)

The sum k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k + 1 is a geometric series:
- If k = 1: sum = n
- If k > 1: sum = (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)

Therefore:
- If k = 1: z = 1 + b × n
- If k > 1: z = k^n + b × (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)

### Step 2: Calculate seconds needed in second experiment

We start with t bacteria and want to get at least z bacteria.

Let f(x) be the number of bacteria after x seconds starting from some initial count.

If we start with initial count `init`, after 0 seconds: init
After 1 second: k×init + b
After 2 seconds: k×(k×init + b) + b = k²×init + k×b + b
After x seconds: k^x × init + b × (k^(x-1) + k^(x-2) + ... + k + 1)

So after x seconds: f(x) = k^x × init + b × (k^x - 1) / (k - 1) if k > 1, or k^x × init + b × x if k = 1

We need the minimum x such that f(x) ≥ z.

We can iterate x from 0 upwards until f(x) ≥ z.

### Step 3: Handle edge cases

- If t ≥ z, then answer is 0
- If k = 1, the formula changes
- We need to be careful with integer overflow, but constraints are reasonable

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
- First experiment: start with 1, after 3 seconds
- After 1: 3×1 + 1 = 4
- After 2: 3×4 + 1 = 13  
- After 3: 3×13 + 1 = 40, so z = 40
- Second experiment: start with 5, want ≥ 40
- After 0: 5 < 40
- After 1: 3×5 + 1 = 16 < 40
- After 2: 3×16 + 1 = 49 ≥ 40
- Answer: 2 ✓

Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
- First experiment: start with 1, each second: 1×current + 4 = current + 4
- After 1: 1+4 = 5
- After 2: 5+4 = 9
- After 3: 9+4 = 13
- After 4: 13+4 = 17, so z = 17
- Second experiment: start with 7, want ≥ 17
- After 0: 7 < 17
- After 1: 7+4 = 11 < 17
- After 2: 11+4 = 15 < 17
- After 3: 15+4 = 19 ≥ 17
- Answer: 3 ✓

Example 3: k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
- First experiment: start with 1
- After 1: 2×1 + 2 = 4
- After 2: 2×4 + 2 = 10
- After 3: 2×10 + 2 = 22
- After 4: 2×22 + 2 = 46, so z = 46
- Second experiment: start with 100, 100 ≥ 46, so answer is 0 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Calculate z from first experiment (starting with 1 bacterium for n seconds)
    if k == 1:
        # When k = 1, each second: current + b
        # After n seconds: 1 + b * n
        z = 1 + b * n
    else:
        # When k > 1: z = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)
        k_power_n = pow(k, n)
        geometric_sum = (k_power_n - 1) // (k - 1)
        z = k_power_n + b * geometric_sum
    
    # Find minimum seconds needed in second experiment (starting with t bacteria)
    current = t
    seconds = 0
    
    # If we already have at least z bacteria
    if current >= z:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Otherwise, simulate second experiment second by second
    while current < z:
        current = k * current + b
        seconds += 1
    
    print(seconds)

solve()
```

In the first experiment:
- Start: 1
- After 1 second: k*1 + b
- After 2 seconds: k*(k*1 + b) + b = k^2 * 1 + k*b + b
- After 3 seconds: k*(k^2 * 1 + k*b + b) + b = k^3 * 1 + k^2*b + k*b + b

So general formula: z = k^n + b * (k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^1 + k^0)
The sum is (k^n - 1) / (k - 1) when k > 1

Yes, that's correct.

Let me verify with the first example again:
k=3, b=1, n=3
k^3 = 27
(k^3 - 1) / (k - 1) = (27 - 1) / 2 = 26 / 2 = 13
z = 27 + 1 * 13 = 40 ✓

Yes, it's correct.

The implementation looks good.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. In the first experiment, we start with 1 bacterium and after n seconds, we have exactly z bacteria.
2. The growth rule: each second, each bacterium divides into k bacteria, then b more bacteria are added.
3. For the second experiment, we start with t bacteria and want to find the minimum seconds to reach at least z bacteria.

First, I need to find z (the target number of bacteria) from the first experiment.

## Step 1: Find the value of z from the first experiment

Given k, b, n, and starting with 1 bacterium, I need to simulate the growth process for n seconds to find z.

The recurrence is: `f(t+1) = k * f(t) + b`

Starting with f(0) = 1:
- After 1 second: f(1) = k*1 + b = k + b
- After 2 seconds: f(2) = k*(k+b) + b = k² + kb + b
- And so on...

So after n seconds: f(n) = k^n + b*(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^1 + k^0)

The sum k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^1 + k^0 is a geometric series:
- If k = 1: sum = n
- If k > 1: sum = (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)

So f(n) = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1) when k > 1
When k = 1: f(n) = 1 + b * n

Let me verify with the first example:
k=3, b=1, n=3
f(3) = 3^3 + 1 * (3^3 - 1) / (3-1) = 27 + (27-1)/2 = 27 + 26/2 = 27 + 13 = 40

But let me trace manually:
- Start: 1
- After 1 sec: 3*1 + 1 = 4
- After 2 sec: 3*4 + 1 = 13  
- After 3 sec: 3*13 + 1 = 40 ✓

So z = 40.

For the second experiment, we start with t bacteria and want to find minimum seconds to reach at least z bacteria.

## Step 2: Simulate the second experiment

We start with t bacteria and apply the same growth rule each second until we reach at least z bacteria.

We need to find the minimum seconds needed.

## Step 3: Consider edge cases

If t >= z, then we need 0 seconds (already have enough bacteria).

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: k=3, b=1, n=3, t=5
z = 40 (as calculated)
We start with t=5, need at least 40
- After 0 sec: 5 < 40, continue
- After 1 sec: 3*5 + 1 = 16 < 40, continue
- After 2 sec: 3*16 + 1 = 49 >= 40 ✓
Answer: 2 ✓

Example 2: k=1, b=4, n=4, t=7
First, find z:
k=1, so f(n) = 1 + b*n = 1 + 4*4 = 17
We start with t=7, need at least 17
- After 0 sec: 7 < 17, continue
- After 1 sec: 1*7 + 4 = 11 < 17, continue
- After 2 sec: 1*11 + 4 = 15 < 17, continue
- After 3 sec: 1*15 + 4 = 19 >= 17 ✓
Answer: 3 ✓

Example 3: k=2, b=2, n=4, t=100
First, find z:
f(4) = 2^4 + 2*(2^4 - 1)/(2-1) = 16 + 2*(16-1)/1 = 16 + 2*15 = 16 + 30 = 46
We start with t=100, which is already > 46, so answer is 0 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    k, b, n, t = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Calculate z - the number of bacteria after n seconds starting from 1
    if k == 1:
        z = 1 + b * n
    else:
        # f(n) = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)
        k_power_n = pow(k, n)
        z = k_power_n + b * (k_power_n - 1) // (k - 1)
    
    # If we already have at least z bacteria with t, we need 0 seconds
    if t >= z:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Otherwise, simulate the growth process second by second
    current = t
    seconds = 0
    
    while current < z:
        current = k * current + b
        seconds += 1
    
    print(seconds)

solve()
```

Wait, let me double-check the geometric series formula.

The recurrence is f(0) = 1
f(1) = k*1 + b = k + b
f(2) = k*(k+b) + b = k² + kb + b
f(3) = k*(k² + kb + b) + b = k³ + k²b + kb + b

So f(n) = k^n + b(k^(n-1) + k^(n-2) + ... + k^1 + k^0)

The sum is (k^n - 1)/(k - 1) when k > 1

So f(n) = k^n + b * (k^n - 1) / (k - 1)

Yes, this is correct.

And for k=1, the sum is n, so f(n) = 1 + b*n.

The simulation part is straightforward - we just keep applying the growth rule until we reach or exceed z bacteria.

The code looks correct!
